LADIES' LETTER

MY DRAB NELLIE,

Toques are now worn and might be in oloth of any hue to match the gown of the wearer, with a plain gold buckle and a dainty soft grebe feather. It is rather a satisfaction to be able to make a declaration at last respecting the headgear that is being worn instead of what is going to be worn. Throughout October we had only promises of novelties to come, but now that November is in we have to deal with actual facts. The weather has been so stormy that the last wisp of light gauze has been wrested from our chapeaux, and we have been obliged in consequence to order something really autumnal for .present-day wear. Panne is very much used in millinery. It is made in all the new pastel tints, and is of so sympathetic a texture that it can be folded into the semblance of flowers under the brim of the hat.

I really like more and more the short coats. The three-quarter garment is a difficult species of wrap in which to walk, for not only does it cause the skirt to cling absurdly about the knees, but it is an impossibility to hold it off the ground. In these particulars a heavy three quarter coat can be more exasperating than a long-trained coat, since with the latter one never attempts to do more than crawl, and leave one's dress to the tender mercies of the dust, whereas the short coat does make one try to walk, and even to lift one's dress from the mud. One I have is of bright red (or huntsman's pink) cloth, piped with a darker shade, the whole decidedly gay, but most attractive for the country daring autumn. The front panel of the skirt is untrimmed, but the sides lie over it in scallops that slope away at the edge of the skirt, and these are piped with the darker red. The front of the bodice is smoothly draped, giving a princess effect, and has dark red cloth buttons on each side.

A dress a friend of mine has *rem very becom- ing. It is created in a perfectly hew cloth called ohevense de la reine, lightly embroidered with gar- lands of silk representing lozenge squares; it has foot frills of velvet, fussy and yet simple, and a draped bolero ef the same fabric held up by ro- settes. With it is worn, when the day demands a wrap, a cape of nearly waist length, with a scal- loped motif of feathers round it rising up to the throat in graduated bands. The millinery suitable for it is deliciously simple—just a felt the colour of the cloth (say the new chestnut), of a perfect shape, trimmed with a twist of velvet. Capes have nob the prominence they erstwhile had, those that are worn being somewhat cumbersome by reason of their length and fulness, though those of fur will still be a good deal worn, very long behind, and shorter in trout, bearing a full and rather sleep flounce.

So many families are thrown into mourning through the loss of their dear ones in the South African war, that black will be largely in evidence for months to come, and the winter season, usually so cheery and gay with its restaurant and theatre parties, and the like, will be shorn of its brightness. Those who have not them- selves lost relatives yet don black, black and white, mauve or grey, as a mark of sympathy with bereaved friends. From the unemotional sartorial point of view this is not to be regretted, as a woman with anything approaching a "com- plexion" always looks her hest in black or neutral tints.

In many of the florists' windows the display of the chrysanthemum at present is superb. So much attention is given by the cultivators and so many growers specialise its culture that it ranks as first favourite for the floral decoration of the home. Its irregular form and depth and variety of colour—important factors—prevent that stiffness which often detracts from an otherwise effective decoration. Any coloured scheme can be carried out for the table, its own leaves providing the requisite relief of greenery, but a better result is obtained by the introduction of leaves of a larger and more distinctive character.

Bunches of Englii!ii sweet violets are beginning to appear now, and soon they will be seen on the baskets of the street flower-sellers. English. grown violets are always preferred, for their per- fume is so much sweeter than that of the French rivals, by _ _

Your loving

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

Although Christmas is some distance off yet, the show of gifts in the shops is considerable, and many have been the purchases made, in readiness tor the " festive season." Royal folks rarely leave their present choosing till the last moment, and lately some of our Princesses have been seen closely examining novelties en evidence as " Christmae gifts" Of late years the Princess or Wales has frequently sent opecisnens of her own handi- work with the camera, as greetings among her own im- mediate circle, and Princess Cnarles of D3nmark often does the same. There are probably few other countries where Royal- ties can walk here, there and everywhere, in the way that they do here. Not long since, I saw the Princess Victoria of Wales, making purchases in a fashionable shop in Mount-street, and the Duchess of York was walking in Bond-street the same morning looking in at the shop windows, and buying things toe. She was very simply dressed, and if recognised, she was certainly not in any way annoyed. Not since the Crimea, or the Indian Mutiny, have the realities of war been so forcibly impressed upon the country as at the present time. Fortunately it is, at the worst, but a very distant acquaintance with its herrora, and not to be compared to the anxiety arid hardships which the war is causing in South Africa,but tbere will be a ereat sense of rebel when the British preparations are sufficiently forward to bring the end in view. It is, as Lord Rosebery has said, "no little war." and it is net surprising that the country is so wrapped up in its, as yet, very up and down fortunes. Not only will Sir Redyers Buller have a larger army under him than was ever before commanded by a British general, but the expedition also exceeds in numbers any force ever be- fore sent away from these shores. The nearest approach to the present military operations was in July 1809,when 41,000 troops were shipped to Antwerp under Lord Chatham. The Army Corps recently mobilised and shipped numbers close upon 47,000 and eventually with all the Regular and Colonial troops, General BuPer will have a force of not less than 90,000 troops at his dis- posal. The Duke and Duchess of Devonshire have been placed in mourning by the death of Commander Egerton, who was mortally wounded in the recent fighting at Lady- smith. He was a nephew of the Duke, and at the time of the engagement was a lieutenant of the Naval Brigade landed from ELM. S. Powerful. He was oely in his thirty-first year,and his death shows that the war claims its victims from all ranks, and all classes. The officers in fact appear to suffer more in proportion to their num- bers, in every successive campaign, and Lord Wolseley says they must expect to suffer. Many of course belong to the highest families, and among those who have lately arrived at the front is Major Princa Christian Victor. One of Lord Salisbury's sons, Lord Edward Cecil, is with Colonel Baden-Powell. It is scarcely more than a year ago since Lord Edward was present with the Sirdar, at the fall of Khartoum, Lord Lan, downe, Lord Tweedmouth, and Lord Ribblesdale, also have sons serving in the war, against the Boers, and Lord and Lady George Hamilton have about a dczen relatives similarly engaged at the Cape. Mrs. Druce. who has long been struggling with the difficulties and delays of the law, with regard to the opening of a certain grave, is still pursuine her claim to the Portland estate, and the other day offered £46,000 worth of Druce Portland bonds for sale at auction with- out reserve. The bonds, according to the undertaking, are to be redeemed at their face value within 12 months of Mrs. Druce's son becoming the Duke of Portland. There was no keen competition for the toads, and from £1 per £lOO bond, the price fell to 2s, and to is. for the £5O bonds. This dees not look very prnmising,but Mrs. Druce announces her intention to offer the bonds all over the country, where people may perhaps take a more sanguine view of her case. To judge however by the result or ending of most of these cases, nothing seems more hopelessly futile than a disputed claim to a peerage There are several such claims being made, of which the Poulett bise recently attracted a good deal of attention. It was stated that a compromise was arrived at in this affair, but of course the Committee of Privileges of the House of Lords would not censent to any compromise as to who should become a member of their own body. The municipal elections, in which the womenfolk utually take no little interest, have this year been com- pletely overshadowed by the war. Imperialism has mostly crowded out all immediate concern for local or domestic matters, but this was not altogether the case in Northampton. At a meeting which Mr. ex Mayor Hanoet was holding, with a view to his re-election, he was asked the following, question by one of the parish- ioners—" If Mr. Hennes happens to be made Mayor again will he not be so severe on a man who happens to give the old woman a thump ?" There could be no possible doubt about what was in the naind ef the ques- tioner, but it is both satisfactory and significant ot the decadence of wife-beating, that the meeting ended by passing a unanimous vote of confidence in the ex-Mayor. It is all very well for coroners and juries to censure poor people who sleep four in a bed, as in a case which formed the subject of a coroner's inquiry quite recently

in a suburb of London- In this case the father was an Arsenal labourer, with nine children, and although it is eminently desirable that people should have plenty ot sleeping space, yet it is pracqcally impossible for a man in such circumstances to do what ha would wish in a district where there is a house famine. It is the London County Council who ought to have been censured for failing to supply municipal dwellings. Toe men who constituted the jury, are, no doubt, in the ordinary affairs of life, siiinsible individuals who take a practical view of things, but although their object was mast cammendable,Te: there may be some question con- cerning their expression of opinion that it should be made criminal for more than one child to sleep with the mother. Do they really believe that such legislation will ever be enacted. It is true, as the jury remarked, that the number of deaths from "accidental "suffoca- tion is enormous, but they are not, as in this case, due to overcrowding. Of every 1,000 de,aths of infants from suffisication in bed 997 are veillui murder, two are due to the drunkenness of the parents and the remainder are pure accidents. The children would be murdered just the same if only one were allowed to sleep with the parents. _ _ _

Fur trimmings are largely en evidence, on up-to-date gowns, but 30 far, the band round the tem is conspicu- ous by its absence, an item to rejoice over. The idea was a messy and extravagant one. Regarding the cloths of which fashionable frocks are now being so.extensively built, these are so soft and smooth that the folds fall with natural grace. Even tweeds and cheviots are of this soft fineness, the wool of which they are made being of the beet. A new cloth is like the skin of some Arctic animal, for thickness. It is called zebelline, and is too thick and warm for wearing in any but the coldest weathar.

" MADGE."

NOBLER THAN REVENGE.

By ESME STUART,

Author of "In the Dark," "Inscrutable," "A. Woman of Forty," "Sara's Mother," Bto.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: On his way to visit his cousin, Sir Harry Beauworth, Alec Beauworth, heir to the title, is set upon by two men and knocked senseless. Sir Harry has a bad reputa- tion. Bob Rabbitts, son of the evil-looking landlord of the Dog and Duck, revives Alec and determines, as he has the chance, to enter Sir Harry's service for a time to see that Alec comes to no harm.

===== 0002601_18991110_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER 111.

The girl wound her arm round her father, and the two moved up the old oak staircase, and went towards the wing of the house set apart entirely for Ruby. This wing turned at right angles to the body of the house. It did not look into the front drive, but into the small park which, at that side, extended as far as the eye could see. The passage was cut off from the rest of the house by a massive oak door which the squire had put up, and it was this door which was locked at night, and the key of which he always carried about with him. Ruby had always been accustomed to see her father lock this door, so that it seemed to her the most natural thing for her and Nancy to be kept safe at night under lock and key. There was only one way out of this wing—by a little door leading down to some unused rooms, but as there was nothing to see there—for the rooms were all darkened with oak shutters —it had never occurred to Ruby to open this door, which merely opened on a dark, narrow staircase. In this way Nancy had a small, upstairs kitchen, which had been fitted up when Ruby had once been taken ill with scarlet fever, and which was now often used if her father was away for days together. To-night, as usual, the father and daughter paused at the great oak door. Sir Hurry put his arm round Ruby's shoulders and drew her head down to kiss her forehead.

"Is there anything you want, Ruby ? " he said. "No, nothing," she answered, slowly. How could she say more, for she did not know what she wanted. '

"Well, good-night, darling; go to bed early, and--"

"And if you can, you will come and see me, to say good-night again ? " "If I can; but to-night I shall be very busy working in the study. Ah, I had forgotten! Here is a little watch which I had sent for, and it only came this evening." It was a small watch, surrounded with diamonds. "0 papa, how lovely! I must shew it to Nancy. How good you are!" Rt,by heard, without noticing it, the key turn in the lock. Then she hurried away to exhibit her new treasure to Nancy. The girl's sitting-room was all ti.at heart could desire. All the panels were carved with quaint devices. There were polished oak sters leading up to the windows, and the room was luxuriously furnished. Nancy sat in a high-backed chair knitting. Her face was very sad, and it certainly needed Ruby's spirits not to feel depressed in Nancy's company. She looked as if she had had some great shock in her life, and had never got over it. "Look, Nancy, what dear old dad has given me! A lovely watch. "It's pretty, Miss Ruby; but what's the use of knowing the time here ? " "0, Nancy, that's what I felt for a moment; but I would not let papa guess it. He does every- thing to make me happy, and yet I seem to want - Oh, what do I want?"

Nancy looked scared. "What did I say, child? Nought—nought to make you feel that—" "Well, if you did, there's no harm," exclaimed Ruby, laughing. "I want to do something I have never done before, or see someone, or— 0 Nancy, what is it? I have you and Leo, and then papa is everything to me. I can't think what makes me like that!"

"You are getting a big wench, dearie, and that's what it is."

"Wench! 0 Nan,gy, you mean a lady; but papa has just said that I am still a child, and must go to bed early." "You do get a load o' loneliness here." "When lam grown-up I shall not go to bed early. Three years more, Nancy I Think of that!" • "What the squire says must be, dearie, so make the best of it; but I would rather be a-" Nancy paused.

"Be 'what ? " "Nothing—nothing, child; get your work and it down whilst I get the supper." Instead of obeying, Ruby went up the steps and gazed out of the window. "There are two men coming across the park, Nancy. Why, it is funny—l've never seen anyone come across that way before. Who can they be?" "Two men," said the old woman, coming for- ward. "Why, no one ever comes‘that way." "Look, Nancy, they are coming over the fence! Papa would be angry if he saw them. I wish we could see their faces.

"No, no, dearie ; don't look. Come away. Your father would be angry." "Angry! What for? Oh, they are strangers that have lost their way. It is raining; they want &leiter." "No, no, dear; let them be. Your father will pe to them." "But they won't know the way by the walled garden, and Leo will bark at them. Oh, I wish I could tell them." _

"You, dearie! Don't you meddle with any men " ; and the old woman muttered, half to herself: "It's what her father said to me many years ago. She is never to see a strange man." Nancy appeared so frightened that Ruby turned round towards her with a puzzled expression on her face. "0 Nancy, you are funny! Papa did not mean that! But I wish we were not locked up. I shall ask him to give us the key. Oh, look! they are coming nearer, and they are looking about. Nancy, do look! Have you ever seen one of them before ? " Nancy looked again, then she pulled down the blind. "No, no; why should I have ever seen them ? " "Oh, but you have! When you say things like that, I know that you mean Yes,' Nancy! I'll tell you what we can de.: run down the stairs of the passage, and there we could speak to them if we open a shutter." "Lack-a-day child, you must be mad! What made you think of such a thing? It would break the wand of peace, indeed, to have you doing such a thing! Even if you went deftly and made no noise, the master would hear you. For Heaven's sake, Ruby, do you want to bring me to trouble? There, the men have gone out of sight, and let them be out of mind. They are but wayfarers." But Nancy had overreached herself to-night. Ruby's mind had been started on a road, which was to bring her to a strange country. "It's no use telling Nancy. She always says' Don't' to every- thing! Why shouldn't Igo down the stairs, and look out of the lower windows? What harm would it do? In that old book I found the other day it said that if a maid went out on a moonlight night and dropped three round, smooth stones down a well, she would get all she wanted. But no one must see the girl go out. Oh, dear! Nancy would see me. Ido wish I could have had a sister or— s lover."

Ruby paused. "Why shouldn't I try? Oh, it would be amusing! Nancy knows something about these men. Odd. Well, I can't do anything now, but next time. Why, it is moonlight now, only the clouds hide the moon. Yes, I'll ask Bob Babbitts, who looks so kind, I'll ask him to bring me three smooth stones, and I'll get out below and drop the stones into the well just outside the garden wall. The lock is broken, I can push it open without being seen. Oh, I'm seventeen! It's time I did wish. What shall it be ? " -

Ruby started up, and made an excuse to fetch a pocket-handkerchief from her bedroom. This room looked the samo way as the sitting- room, but it had two windows—one, which was never opened, looked out towards the front, as it was in the angle. If Ruby could open this, she could see if the two men did come to the house.

—.— She opened her own window, and looked out into the cold and rain, listening intently. Leo would bark if they were strangers; but no, Leo never lifted up his voice. Suddenly she heard the garden gate pushed open: and steps pass into the drive, then turn into the angle, where she could not follow them. She tried to open the little window, but it was firmly fixed. "I believe," said Ruby to herself, and for the first time in her life, "I believe I am a prisoner. Why? "

Without waiting for an answer Bob seized the lanthorn, and the stranger heard him softly scrambling overhead. Still, to his ears, nothing was audible. This was certainly an extraordinary adventure. He, Alec Beauworth, a poor, unknown architect's clerk in London, had received a letter from his unknown cousin, Sir Harry Beauworth, asking him to come and pay him a visit at Yewdale Hall. Alec's parents had died before he could remember, and he had been cared for by a lady, who, having made the acquaintance of his mother during the last month of her life, had, at her death, taken pity on. the poor baby, who had nothing left to him but his life. Miss Grey had been a mother to him, but her small income was but an annuity, and had died with her. She had saved a few hundred pounds for her adopted son, and with this he had started himself in an architect's office. Here he had but distant prospects of fame and advance- ment. His mother had left behind her some letters which had always interested Miss Grey, but of which Alec, with his broader views, had thought little. These letters contained proofs that he was the next in succession to the Beauworth estates, if young Beauworth died without children, and if the estate was not claimed by the boy's uncle, who had gone abroad and had been lost sight of, and who was most probably dead. The young man was not likely to marry, as he vas not as bright as he should have been, but as Alec had often pointed out to his adopted mother, the uncle was most likely alive, and, if so, he would be a fool not to turn up and claim his rightful possessions. "But your mother's letter says he, too, was very delicate when he started for some wild region in Australia. I feel sure he is dead."

Just before Miss Grey's death the young Beau- worth died, and the kind spinster felt convinced her Alec was at last provided for, but very soon the lawyers sent word that Sir Harry Beauworth had telegraphed from abroad that he had seen the death of his nephew, and that he was coming with his daughter by the next ship. "Then he has only a daughter, and you are still the heir. I wish I could live to see you Sir Alec,' my boy, instead of leaving you to struggle with fate."

When Miss Grey died Alec felt very lonely, but he was fond of his profession, and worked hard with- out giving many thoughts to the heirship of Yew- dale. He heard that Sir Harry was by no means too old to marry again, for he must desire an heir of his own. Ten years paFsed and Alec, now thirty-one years old, had almost fogotten his rela- tions, when, one morning, he received a letter from Sir Harry, asking if he would give him the pleasure of a visit, as he wished to confer with him on important matters. Alec managed to get a week's leave, wishing it were summer tiine instead of cold winter, but any break in the routine of his daily life was a relief, and he started out with a light heart for the lonely place with which he was totally unacquainted. And now, instead of being an honoured guest at the Hall, here be lay hidden like a thief, after having been knocked .down by some drunken fellow. Well, it was an ignominious beginning to a visit, and the squire would certainly look upon him as a poor specimen of manhood, who could not reach his destination without encountering ridiculous adventures. THE LORD MAYOR'S SHOW.

For L'mdoners there is no need to describe the Lord Mayor's Show. For others we may say that it is a pro- ceesion which is to be seen every 9at November, when the new Lord Mayor takes offict,, and proceeds, attended wy the Aldermen, Sheriffs, and Common Council- men, with bands of music, aed with banners flying of al the City GoiLis, to thn Exchequer Court to bo sworn ha. Then, at night, follows the Lord Mayor's ',annual banquet, in the Guild Hall—a mighty deed of drinking and feeding. Every year since 1501. have these gustatory festivities been held there—about 400 of them altogether. What beasts of the field and birds of the air have gone to the manufacture of that long series of official feasts. Alas! for rime's tatal t ouch. They have passed hate Limbo, and 80 have most of the men that ate them. Soletra and instructive thought!

And they ran into money 'withal. The cos of the Lord Mayor's Show, dinner included, is about £3,500, of which his lordship pays half and each sheriff one-fourth. Surely the Lord Mayor hath ways to .spend his £lO.OOO a year—even if it is twice the salary of the Prime Minister. Not all the people who march in the Lord Mayor's procevion get a chance to put their legs under the tables at the Lord Mayor's banquet. Oh, no—nor an; thing like it. The elephants and camels are oinitted, of course; and the policemen, the eoldiere, the iiadorr, beefeaters, etc., must content thernselver, with their ordinary rations, and a good j.b for them too. Because the cuetti at the big feed will eat too nauo'a and drink too much. They always do. And then the miseries of the day after Sharp indig,estions, parizaysms of biliousness, awful " heads " arid collapsed nerves are on the list. The Habitual Dyspeptics—at least half the honourable and discinguieheci company— groan under the onset of their Intimate Enemy and wished they had stopped outside with the parties who were not invited. But the remedy that pulled them through last year (Mother S 'igen Syrup) soon sets matters to rights. THE EXAMINEP„ FRIDAY,

The Suicide at Latchford.

Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest at the Polled Station on Saturday on the body of Patrick Quinn, Manor House, Lower Wash-lane Latchford, who was found hanging from a nail in the scullery ceiling by bis wife on Friday.— Catharint Quinn, wife of deceased.said her husband was forty-four years of age, and was an engine driver at Mr. Parkinson's tannery. Ile was a very steady man but lately had complained of dizziness and du)lness of the eyes. On Friday afternoon at four o'clock, she went to take tea to the works which were near the house and on her return tound him haliging on a nail in the scullery ceiling. When cut down Le was dead.—A verdict of " Suicido whilst of untounl mind" was returned.

NOTlCE.—Solvers who become entitled to a prize in this comp3tition can, if they desire, have any book on the game of Draughts equal to the value of ss. instead of cash prz,?, by notifying the Draughts Editor.

In connection wirh the above competition we may state that we offer a monthly prize of 5s to the competitor who succeeds in placing himself at the head of the Solvers' List at the end of the month. Where the prize is awarded the winner's score is cancelled, while the remaining competitors' points go towards the next month's competition. The win- ner may re-enter the competition if he chooses commencing again at the bottom of the list. Points are awarded as followi, : Two for the correct solu- tion of a problem, two for showing a dual solution, two for correcting the author's terms, and one for each minor correction. Inteiading competitors may commence at any time.

We have t,3 announce that our Competition for October resulted in a tie between Mr. W. Rigby, 18, General-street, and Mr W. Rewitt,but on the ballot being resorted to according t 3 rules, Mr. Rigby was pronounced the winner. GAM ES.

===== 0002601_18991110_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
The Church Crisis.

The Rev. F. E. Powell, vicar of St. James' Church. Latchford, continued his course of sermons on the pre- sent " Church Crisis" en Sunday. His subject in the morning was " Is there a sacrifice in the holy com- munion 2' and at night" What is meant by s'acerdotal- ism ?" Speaking upon the last-named he saii The dread of Popery is not in itself uereasonable. If we felt that there was the remotest chance of a return of our church and country to the deadening and blighting conditions of Popery, no protest—so long at it was reasonable and dignified—could be considered too strong. We have only to look at the condition of the countries where Popery is still in vogue. Take the four leadine countries in Europe which are spiritually bound to the Papacy—France, Italy, Spain and Austria. Take the South American republics where Roman Catholicism is the chiA religious force. The moral and spiritual condition of these countries does not inspire us with any desire for Popery. Therefore as against the unlawful and unscriptural claims of the Bishop of Rome, as against the accretions to the Catholic faith which differentiate Romanism from Catholicism, we may and should be soundly Protestant. But we cannot give up that which can be hi-torically and theologically defined as Catholic because the uo- mess ming prejudices of extreme Protestantism choose to regard it as Popiat. If there are those who are unable to see the difference it is not our fault but their misfor- tune. The Bible, in both Old and Nsw Tes-aenent, teaches that every member of the Church of Gad is a priest to offer up spiritual sacrifices acceptable to God. If therefore all Christians are priests it is a little hard to exclude the officers of the Church and to say that they are not priests. The lay-priesthood and the ministerial priesthood differ not in that one offers sacrifices and the other does not,but that all are called to offer sacrifices to God—the clergy "in things pertaining to Go in it- ' ters of religion and az orship,the laity in matters of life. A man who says he want; no one to come between his soul and God should never read the Bible, for the Holy Ghost spake through the prophets, and translators and revisers and printers, and publisnere, and booksellers have come between his soul and God whenever he reads that timed Book for edifilation In both Church and State the powers that be, are ordained of God— exercising the authority which resides throneh Gad n the whole body of the State or of the Church. Of course if any should teach that there is no intercourse of the spirit of man with the Almighty Father without human ministry, such teaching cannot ba too strongly con- demned.

===== 0002601_18991110_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
The Boers and Mr. Gladstone.

Preaching in St. James' Church, on Sunday evening, the Rev. F. E. Powell, the vicar, said: I think it only right, s.min: that our minds and hearts are is o full of the war now being waged in South Africa, that some thing should be said in this pulpit concerning it. When my present course of sermons was arranged and announcr.d war had not been declared, and we in England hoped and prayed that a pacific settlement of the unhappy controversy which had arisen might be obtained. Unfortunately it was not to be. The quarrel is not 50 mucls about words or even deeds, but about feeling, and as to whether the Beer ideal or the English ideal shou'd prevail throughout South Africa. It is no false patriotism which has made us insist that the British ideal shall prevail. We believe it will be better for the whole of South Africa that its civilization and constitution should te based on English principles and methods, rather than on those of the Boers. There is of course much in the enemy to respect. The B )ers are frugal, thrifty, industri,us, and for the most part upright men. We know too that they are bravo and determined. But the demands made upon them by our Government have not been unreasonable. They practically amount to this : Treat the English in your country as we English treat the Dutch in ours. There is one point which it seems to me has not re- ceived the attention it meritP. To my mind the saddest 1 feature of this terrible strife is the failure of Mr Glad- stone's noble and magnanimous treatment of the Boers 1 after the battle of Majuba Hill. I have always thought that treatment—l think it still—one of the very noblest acts of statesmanship in the history of the human race. It was the attempt to deal as a nation with another nation on those Christian principals by which individuals are urged to treat individuals. The failure is deplorable for it will make it very difficult for another statesman of Mr Gladstone's moral calibre to act similarly again. By their conduct towards the English aftsr the generous consideration they had rec4v d 1.8 years ago, the Boers it JAMS to me have shown the world their contempt for a statesmanship which was based on Christian principl .. And this Barely is the most deplor- able, the very saddest feature of the whole sad business. In the meanwhile we may hope and pray that a better understanding will prevail, that Eaglish add Dutch hav- ing learned mutual resrect,will settle down as amicably as the British and French have done in Canada,and that a new era of peace and brotherhood may dawn on South Africa. And the heroic conduct of our brave eoldiers, officers and mon on the field of battle, and the splendid moral cour age—of greater value than even his great physical daring—of Sir George White—will, we trust, react upon our beloved country and sqmolate ira Iv al the same spirit of devotion to dur,y, of loy 1,1:y and patriotism, above all of self-sacrifice in all we believe to be high and true and good. Rev. E. R. Tarbuck on the War.

The Rev E B Tarbeck preaching .at St. Paul's Church. on Sunday evening, took for his text the words" 0 Israel, return unto the L thy God ; for thou bast fallen by thine iniquity. Take with you words, and turn to the Lord ; say *lntl Him, take away all iniquity, and receive us graciously ; so will WA render the calves of our lips. Asshur shall not save us ; we will not ride upon harsee ; neither will we say any more to the work of our hards, ye are our Gals; for in thee the fatherless findeth :nercy " Hosea xiv. verses 1, 2 and 3. The rev. gentleman, at the outset, remarked that in a time like this, when the English world was experiencing the gravest anxiety as to the wel- fare of a very large body of our represerrative soldiers, it might well be suitable that they should consider the value of prayer. In the Church's acheme of salvation prayer was e,sentially t Liking with Gil. ro use a military phrase, it ve.is an A4s•ntial part of tiat system by whi 1h the army in action kept in close touch with headquarters. Th a army in altion, the militant army, was constantly sending home word of its difficulties, de- feats, victories, needs of assistance in man, in feed, in guiiance, in weapons, &I. Lad a. in an ideal country, these communications from the fighting front received the greaCeet attention and the most praJtical and enemaraging replies from headquarters, so each army of the Church Militant, each suairen or Latta- lion, each troop or company, each private soldier re- ceived from and through the spiritual commander-in-thief all things nezessary to victory, except Viet which each

soldier was assumed to have when be enlisted, namely. spirit of determinatinn to conquer the foes of his commander, and even for the asking that spirit might be refined and hardened. The preaeher roterre.l to the disaster which betel Colenel Carleton's column at Nicholson's Nek on the 30th October and discussed several of the reasons which had been put forth. Now what was the reason that satisfied the English mind that someone had blundered ? It was just thia, Mat there was no keeplng open of the lines of communication. Na one seemed to have arranged for a troop of cavalry to go bet ween the bead quarters and the moving column—there was no communication with the head commander, Sir George White. Not only so, but the column se trled Co have been forgotten altogether,as far as one could see; and yesterday, in one of the public papers,a very clever critic sugges*.ed that if that column had carried with it a carrier pigeon in a short time another column could have been sent to Its support. We gave in at three o'clock when our ammunition was exhausted. Even an Engli,hman could not fight with- out ammunition. The column was is )latel,awav from its head quart.rs, dependent on itself,and it fell. No doubt later on full details of the terrible dislster would be supplied to the world that would not bring a blush of shame to Englishmen who would read that the struggle was a long and painful one, comrade after comrade f ail- ing by the side of thcse who would remain to tell the sad details. That was an incident that explained jest what he wanted to point out—the power and value ot prayer, the necessity of keeping open the line of com- munication between the soldiers of Christ at .the front and the great spiritual :Commander-in-chi,f at head- quarters. Our cause was, he contended, a just one, tending to justice, peace and prosperity, and that as they believed their cause was just they could consistently pray for success to their arms in the Transvaal. It was their privilege that night to show their practical gratitude to the army by contributing to tee support of the families left behind. The Vicar and wardens would decide through which channel the money should go, but they would so allocate their money, that the local claims came first, and of those the claim of the widow and fatherless should have the largest share.

The Congregational 20th Century Fund.

" The Coner-eeatioual Twentieth Century Fund : Its Reason' and its Obj-cts wee; the subject of a special discourse by the Rev. J. Yonge in Wycliffe Church, Bewsey-street, on Sunday evening. He stated that in the majority of C magregational Churches in the kingdom that day the subject of the 20:M Century Fund, in one way or another, was being considered. The inception of 20th Century Funds in connection with the various religious bodies was an evidence of the desire in Christian hearts to meet the requirements of the coming age in the spirit of Christian courage and enterprise. l'he honour of the first bugle call to this advance movement belonged, he believed, to the Wesleyan Church, which, largely at the suggestion of e conspicious layman had stepped forth with enthusiasm tc the tune of" A Million Guineas Fund." They were steadily progressing to the realisation of their scheme. l'he example thus given had operated upon other Christian leadies, so that there had since been s:arted 20th Century Funds, of varying arneunts, by several other of the Methodist bodies, by the Baptists, and by other churches. In the Congregational deaominatian, Dr. Guinness Rogers—the veteran stalwart of their cleurctres—had made it his latest and not least exacting work to inspire and inaueurate the 20:11 Century fund. His first idea was a quarter of a. million guineas. En- couraged by the reception it received, he speedily doubled the figure, and made it half a million. There were some who believed that, under the latest expansion of the plan, even the figure of the half million might be far exceeded. Certainly it was the case that, as this groat scheme had been made known to their churches in all parts of the Kingdom, small churches equally with Larger ones had accepted it with enthusiasm. It was true that they at Wycliffe stood committed to a very considerable scheme of their own. It had been found— and it was a good and healthy sign—that most churches were laden with schemes and committed to expenditures of special kinds. An idea, therefore, had been hit upon by which this twentieth century movement would take over these schemes, r, ckon them in its own fund, on the gear al understanding that churches thus affected should promise to contribute to the great central fund, five or ten per cent upon their expenditure upon the local object. Tani would enable them to complete the Rigby memorial, and also gain this fund and have at least a share in promot- ing the great objects which it contemplated. The reasons for the 20th Century Fund were supremely unselfish ; they were patriotic; they were Cnristian, and they were urgent. The first reason stated for their favourably considering the scheme was " We have had a century of unprecedented National prosperity." That fact admitted of no dispute. The 19th century for this country bad been a period of growing and even unrivalled prosperity. There had doubt- less been fluctuations ; there had even been times of great depression. There had been experiences and there were still social and industrial conditions that were to be lamented and deplored, huttany person who studied the condition of things in this country to-day and com- pared it with a century ago, could only be astonished at the tremendous advance in social, commercial, indus- trial and political achievement. No doubt there was a vast amount of soeial distress still existent , Pauperism and poverty still darkened our p opulatioas. , The contrast between West Ends and East Ends still re- mained a source of sorrow and in some senses perhaps , even a danger to the whole community. But who could I dispute the better state of such a society as that repre- sented at Wycliffe to-day, compared with what would have been their conditions a hundred years ago. They had greater social privileges, greater domestic enjoy- ments, larger persenal rights an 1 pesseesiona. Taey had better houses, more means, better clothes, better furni- ture, better hours of work, better opportunities for mental development, greater facilities tor recreation, more chance of commercial progress, more rights and libertieo fewer disabilities, fewer deprivals. The Kingiom:andEmpire ban developed by leaps and bounds and they had sharad in its advantages. Now, they needed to realize that to those to whom much was given, much was required and if a gracious Providence had put it within the present pessib lity of Congregational Churches to-day to do more than in the past for Christ and Christianity, through tbe possession of larger re- sourca,then it followed that their churches should show themselves as willing as they were able for the conse- cration and service that was required. A second lesson why their Congregational Churches should rally to that scheme was gratitude for the progress of civil and religious liberty which had marked the century now closing. The young people to-day had very little idea of the experiences of Nonconformists a hundred years ago. It was then, for example customary for dbseciters tope deemed the Party of Disloyalty ; traitors to the State and Destroyers of the Faith. Any excuse was good enough to attack them with vielence and to invent measure; of repression and persecution. Lip to the year UM, it was illegal for any person hold- ing judicial, civil or municipal office te attend a Noncon- formist place of worship wearing the gown or robe of office or with the insignia such as mace and sword belonging to the same. Sine.e that year, such proceedings might take place in any place of worship. He noted that simply because it would be b.fore them in actual illus- tration in a few days, and he noted it in no sense as being a triumph over the Establishment but inmply as an achievement of a public right and liberty which adorned the history of Free Churchiein in the century now c'osine. They ware living in a wonderful day. it was a time of deep interest in the religious world. Many things were happening. Some of them might be sugges- tive of anxiety, but more of them, he thought. were suggestive of hope. Men were as pronounced as ever upon their conscientioae beliefs. But they were becom- ing more charitable. With better knowledge of each other, they were getting to kimdlier sympathy and to more genial charity. To the thire reason, they could do little more than briefly point. It was however a remarkable fact that the closing century had been" one of unexampled religious acti- vity." That was true of all the churches. It had been the missionary century, and b .side that, the churches had been busy in home evangelisation. Every body of , Christians with hardly an exception, had developed arid increased. Not that they had kept equal pace with the increase of population. And not,alas,that the end of the century could record a triumphant capture of the popu- lation for Christ. Itedeed if the population were cap- tured for Christ in this country, the problem would be where to house them in the wale of the churches. Their " Warrington Examiner" had a paragraph, which he would quote. It said" It has bean ascertained that if all the inhabitants of the Britisn Isles were seized with a desire to attervi a place of worship, 25\ millions would be crowded out ! Taking the buildings beloneine to every denemina'ien there is only space to seat 15,000,000. The population is 40,000000 ; therefore if all wanted to attend worship, the accommodation would have to be three-fifths larger than it is at present." They as Con- greeationalists bad not been as expansive, as aggres- sive, as devoted as they (anent to be. Ceneregational- ism in Warrington ought, by this time to have taken much larger port in the welfare of the people than as yet had been the case. There should he a second or even a third Congregational Church in this neighbour- hood and the 20th century must see the achievement of this in its very early veers. The preacher then referred to the fifth reason" We must desire to begin the new century in the spirit of self-sacrifice, and with the de- termination to meet ins deepest needs," in which he stated that it must not he thought that this great saheme had been cosceived in any spirit of denomina- tional ambition, of ecclesiastical pride and self se 'king. He was confident that the inspiring motive had been to turn to best account the great occasion of the dawn of a nevr century. In conclusion the sneaker spoke of the objects of the fund, viz., for church and school exten- sion, missions in the Colonies and in foreian lands, ex- tension of crippling debts, Ruppert of poor struggling churches and pasfers in rural districts, founding of new churches in great centres, better suppert of colleges, schools and institu' iona. For the benefit of these the sch.me was to raise half a inillioa guineas between now and Aueust 1901.

A WEST OF ENGLAND WORTHY.

Characteristic Story, A " Special " in the " Bridport News " gives a ch4racteristic history of West County worthy. James Hawker, of Uploderr, Dorset. Engaged in farming,Mr. Hawker was seen at hia home, and racily answered the proposal to publish a part of his life-history. " Lor' bless you, sir. I mant everyone to know !" he said ; and it was an interesting story that the skilful reporter e/Cracted from him. -4 Like most agriculturists, Mr. Hawker had to have his grumble. Times were not over good ; the weather was trying ; he had not been very well, etther—" In fact, I used to be terribly bad, what with my rheumatism and bleeding at the nose ; oh, for eighte-n months or more, and not a week without it. But I've not had it now since the beginning of March." " Ah," said Mrs. Hawker from the corner, " Jim's another sort of a chap now. Be used to have the rheu- matism so bad he couldn't lift his arms or dress him- self." The story was fairly start-d now, and the re- porter had only to take it down in rapid shorthand. " Ay, that's right enough. 1 bad the rheumatism off and on, you know, Nr three or four year, and had to stop at home. Work ! how cauld a fellow work when you can't lift your arms up? But I did suffer, I can tell 'se" .

Mrs. Hawker again interrupts the conversation. "But, you know, sir, I sa w something in one ot the little books about rheumatism being cored by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, and as poor Jim couldn't got anything to ease him. I thought he should try the Pills, as it said they were good for that. He'd tried nearly everything and beer doctoring for years." " Well, did he take them r "Take 'em ! Yes ; but I looked on it more as a joke than anything, and said to the miseis I'd ;like the box and all. " Ab, Jim laughed at me, but it turned out right. I used to have a job with him, and got anything I could. I got him some embrocation, but nothing did him any good, and I began to feel he never would ba right. I got anything I could, and it was a blessing I saw that little book about Dr. Williams' pink pills." "That's right enough, and you can't think how I feel about those pills. I felt easier after the first dose. I said. By love, miss's, I believe they're the thing for me now. for I seem looser about the shoulders already !' That was a day or two after I began taking 'em, and so I went on, and, would you believe it, when I had taken two boxes and a half I was as right and as well as ever I was. I'd been bad for years, and some mornings I couldn't heave my arms up to my head. I hardly knew myself when I got rid iaf the pain. The change the pills brought about was wonderful, and I tell everybody about it, because I think if folks knew more about 'em it would be better tor 'ens, and save a lot of pain and expense in doctoring." The mother hero said, "Ah, Jim, it wasn't only the rheumstism, but they stopped the nose-bleeding." Mrs. Hawker said that was so,as her husband couldn't stoop to chop a stick without his nose bleeding." "And you know, sir, she addad, "I got frightened about it, because I've heard it brings on fits. Besides, he was never safe anywhere, and it kept him so weak. If I have anything wrong with me I take some of Dr. Williams' pink pills, and they soon pull me together

again." It is upan such evidence as this that Dr. Williams' pink pills have built up their immense reputation. Merit alone has made them a household word: people have used them and told each other of their value. Conse- quently there is hardly a village and certainly not a town in England where inquiry will not bring to light some local celebrity who has become the talk of the ueighbouthood, thnugh being cured as if magic- ally by Dr. Williams' pink pills. Along with the severe cases cured (consumption, paralysis, chronic rheumatism, gout and so forth) go thousands cif milder Cases, where such ailments as erysipelas,scrofula, eczema, St. Vitus' dance, neuralgia, and an nia have bean cured, or wh,re weak. languid invalids have grow well and strong, thanks to Dr. William" pink pills. The only danger is that substitutes are sometimes pushed by unscruoulous shopkeepers. The genuine pills are safe, and are perfectly free frem all purgative effect. Sub- stitutes may be dangerous, and they have never been shown to cure anyone. T'ne way to avoid them is to deal with honest tradesmen who do nit try ti sell you sub- stitutes ; to ask carefully for Dr. Williams pink pills for pale people, and take care to see that all these words are printed on the wrapper of the pills ; to take no notice of anything that may be said about substitutes beine " as good " or " the same" as Dr. Williams' (which is wickedly untrue); and if there is any doubt, seed direct to Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, Holborn Viaduct. London, enclosing the prica—two and ninepence for one box ; thirteen and nine for six boxes.

POLLUTION OF THE SHIP CANAL. Complaint from the Vicar of Thelwall. A meeting of the Mersey an I Irwell Joipt Com- mittee was held on Monday at the offices of the Committee in Mosley-street, Manchester, Sir J. T. Hibbert presiding, Mr. Harcourt E. Clare, clerk to the County Council of Lancashire, was appointed clerk to the committee at a salary of .£6OO per annum subject to the conditions of the late Mr. Hulton's engagement; It was stated that complaints had been made concerning the objectionable odours coming from the Ship Canal. On this point the Chief Inspector reported as follows :—" That these complaints are fully justified there can be no doubt, as the coadition of the river and the canal during the dry weather of the past summer has been very bad. As the Committee are aware, there is still a considerable amount of raw sewage discharged into both the Irwell and Mersey, in ad- dition to which the effluent discharged by other authorities who only partially treat their sewage contribute largely to the nuisance. THE FATAL SHUNTING ACCIDENT AT SANKEY.

Inquest and Verdict. Mr. S. Briehous-., county c)roaer, neet an invest at the Warrington Police Station on Saturday toucning the death of John Cawley, 38 years of age, who died on the way to the Infirmary oe Thursday night. Deceased was in the employ of Messrs. Clare and Rideway, slate and lime merchants, an i at 6.15 on Thursday he and another man named Manning were engaged in sheeting a waggon on the siding. Daceas d had fastened the lass rope and was stepping away, when another waggon shunted up and he was crushed between the buffers.— Mr. W. J. Forahaw appeared ter Messra. Clare and Ridg- way, and Mr. Bland for the L. and N.-W. Railway Com- pany.—The first witness was Elizabeth Cawley. She said that deceased was her husband and resided in Heod-lane, Sankey. lie was a general labourer in the employ of Messrs. Clare and Ridgway and was thirty-eight years of age last birth- day. William Manning, 6, Clare-street, Sankey Bridges, a carter in the employ of M scare. Clare and Ridgway re- lated the circumstances of the accident. Before he and deceased commenced to load the waggon he was perfectly satisfied in his own mind that at the time of the. accident all the shunting hei been done for the day. —By Mr. Forshaw ; They were both loading cement.— By Mr. Bland ; It was daylight when the accident occurred. Further questionee, he said it was "going dusk a little." No signal or warning was given. I Mr. Bland said th ac the shunting had not finished at the time of the accident. Toe Railway Company were on private premises and it was for Messrs. Clare and Ridgway to look after the safety of their men. The Coroner said it eyes the duty of the London and North Western Railway to see that the shunting was per- forea.d with safety to the men at work. Mr. Bland: We did what was necessary under the circumstances. We had previoeslv received instructions to shunt, and Messrs. Clare and Ridgway had full notice of the shunting. Thomas Whelan. in the employ. of the Mersey White Lead Company, said a signal ef warning might have bees given. ha: he di I not hear it. William Henry Brady, Cumberland-otreet. Latchford, in the employ of the London and North Western tied- way Company as a brake= an, said that shunting was commenced at 4-25. There were then three waggons in Clare and Ridgwas'a siding, and they were separate and he had to couple them up sad take them out. Witness deleribed the method of ahunting. H shouted "Are you all clear there ? He always did so when in a strange siding. The engine gave one whittle, and earns in at a slow pace. • He only spoke to the deceased once, and that was during a heavy shower of rain,whea they were sheltering. He never told the deceased that shunting operations had finished for the day.—By Mr. Fovhaw : They had been shunting for abut an hour and there was an interval of twenty minutes before the accident occurred.—By Mr. Bland : He would do no shunting without Clare and Ridgway's sanction. Inter- vals in tho shunting had occurred before. Be had had incasien to complain of a number of rasa in the service of Messes. Clare and Ridgway keeping at their work while shunting was going on. He had been unnerved on more than one occasion by it. He had complained to Mr. Ritlway &bent the matter.—By the Coroner : He was not aware the deceased was between the waggons at the time of the accident. The Foreman and several of the jury expressrd the opinion that the constructioa of the yard was such that a shout would not be heard. In answer t a juryman witness said he did not pre. some anyone was at work when he shouted. The Coroner said he was quite satisfied that there had been no criminal negligence on the part of anyone. Mr. Forshaw ; lam also quite satisfied that there was no such negligence. The Coroner : It is only a question of 'civil liability between Clare and Ridgway and the deceased's rela- tives,and I am not going to thrash that out in this Court. I don't think you (the jory) ought to burden the issue of another court by expressing an opinion about it, and if you do it is not made use of in the other courts. The jury after consultation returned a verdict of ("Accidental death."

The Coroner : Th it does not say there is no one to blamo from a civil point of view. It does not relieve anyone from civil liability. It only means that the case is devoid of criminal responsibility. Mr. Bland : I perfectly understand that. The proceedings then terminated.

Accident at Wilderspool Brewery.

About twelve o'clock on Monday morning, a man named William Owen, of Old London-road, Stockton Heath, was working on a hoist at the Wilderspool Brewery when he fell to the ground, a distance of about 26, feet. He was brough-, to the Warrington Infirmary where he was found to be suffering from general bruises about the body.

TEN THOUSAND POLICYHOLDERS AT THE FRONT.—AI though those who have given any thought to the sub- ject would expect to find a fair proportion ot the soldiers of the Queen included in ths ranks of the policyholders of the Prudential Assurance Company, it is astonishing in the highest degree to learn that in a very short time now no less than 10,000 of the Company's clients be facing the enemy. The extra risk on this imtneuse body —an army in It-4elf—is very real, as is evidniced by the fact that the Directors have already paid claims sport the lives of soldiers who fell at Glencoe and Elandslaagta Nevertheless, actuated by sentiments which will be apo preciated [la 0:ily by those who are doing toe fighting, but much more so by the dependents and relatives at home, the Directors have decided not to impose any ad. ditional premium for war risk in the case of industrial policyholders at tie, front. The generosity of the Directors does not stop here. It appears that tnauv of the Company's agents liave been called out wt h the reserves, and arragagements have been made to hold their positions open to theau on their return, and to allow half their salaries to their wives whiCe they are away on duty with their regiments. These concessions will. we take it, be generally approved of, and no doubt the good exampl thus set will be speedily followed in other guarters.—" Commercial Waxld."

WHATEVER may be the views we The Country's Generosity. hold about the war, we must all

- admire the generous way in which the country is coming forward to relieve the im- mediate wants of the direct sufferers by operations in the field. There has been nothing like it be- fore. During the Crimean War no effort was made to provide money for the wives and families of the soldiers, who had been killed or taken pri- soners. It was not until the war was over that the Patriotic Fund was set going in every part of the country,and even then the response was hardly so general and so enthusiastic as it promises to be now. There are many reasons to account for the change. The events of the war were not followed so closely, because telegraphed despatches were impossible, and Dr. Russell's letters to. the "Times "only came at intervals, and did not even then reach all classes, as present messages are do- ing. There were no Reservists in those days, fall- ing back into civil employment and marrying, hardly expecting to be called up again to serve with their regiments. The country was not so prosperous, the causes of the war were not so intelligible, and martial spirit had not been so developed, by volunteering and the like.. It is quite pleasant to think that we can afford to be generous, in spite of political differences, and that we have reached the sensible conclusion that we must not leave everything to the tender mercies of the Government. The money which is being subscribed is mainly to prevent the wives and families of dead soldiers from going on the parish. It is lamentable to think that this has often had to be the case in times past. It might be so even now, when the Government is disposed to be more liberal-banded, were it not for private benevolence. However, there will be little neces- sity for anything of the kind just now. Ample funds are coming in, and if there are local com- mittees in regimental districts there is no reason why there should not be early and efficient relief It appears that it is quite within the province of Boards of Guardians to contribute to such funds, with the sanction of the Local Government Board. There is no case on record of such sanction being asked for,:but in the cases where men have been taken prisoners from one district, leaving their wives and families without help—because no pay is received until the captivity is over—the Guardians might very well make a donation to any local fund. The recipients of money from such a fund would not be pauperised. We are much averse to creating paupers of persons who have been affected by Imperial policy, and now the way out of the difficulty is so clear it ought to be taken firmly and promptly. The French people must be struck by the timeliness with which the generous people of the country have taken up the "wives and families 'S question. Large employers of labour and large boiios of working-men are giving according to their means for the relief of sufferers. The contributions in aid of the wounded in the war will be very helpful, and they promise to be very considerable. All wars are horrible, and wars between white men are especially des- tructive and terrible. But we can do much to lessen the suffering. Armies in the field do not burn aa..d ravage as in the bad old days, and the stay-at-homes, who have doubts about the war, and who distribute their hla.me for it with no fal- tering hand, can still contribute of their means to lessen the suffering, misery, and destitution which follow in its wake.

THE visit of the CZAR te the

At Potsdam. _ laisEß, at Potsdam this week cannot be passed over as an event of purely personal courtesy. It is of some importance in the general European situation. The old friend- ship between the two countries has never been wholly forgotten, though there have been some breaks and a little commercial estrangement of a temporary character. The Russians are apt at times to concentrate Cossacks on the German border without meaning anything by the operation. It is part of the scheme of military evolutions which Russia engages in every now and then. Similar concentrations have taken place on the Austrian border without having any threatening significance. The KAISER and the CzA.n cannot do better than clearly understand each other, in Eastern as well as in Western European affairs. They pursued a common policy for a time in Armenian, Cretan, and Greek affairs, though their aims were not always intelligible to outsiders. Probably they have an agreement, or will be able to establish one, about Asia Min or and Turkey in Europe. It is said that there is an understanding between Germany and England about Asia Minor, which gives to the former some religious, if not ulterior and territorial, advantages. It may be that the two potentates discussed these matters this week amd came to an agreement about them. Germany has been reorganising and improving the Turkish Army, and we saw some of the results in the war in Thessaly. It is not clear that these proceed-

ings were necessarily hostile to Russia, Events appear to show that until a more convenient sea- son there has been an abandonment of the desire to occupy Constantineple and to make of Turkey a Russian province. If it were not so we do not :Ice how Russia could have looked on complacently, as it is clear she has done, whilst her old foes were being armed with modern weapons and instructed in the art of war by German officers. Hence there may be little to discuss bearing upon Eastern Europe, or it may be deferred until the gathering at Skierniewice. But in Western politics there must be many things the two Emperors may use- fully review. Events in France do not favourably impress the cool observer, and the Russo-Yrench alliance, if it has seemed to give France more contentment, has not smoothed away internal political difficulties. The greater the friendliness that exists between the two Emperors the less the likelihood of France receiving much encourage- ment to break the peace. Some of the Continental journals think that South African affairs mast come under review. But the two Powers have absolutely no concern in the matter. The quarrel is of a domestic nature, and neutrality is being observed by them in a way to which no inter- national lawyer can take exception. The KAISER'S visit to the Quilinf is not likely to be referred to in other than the most friendly terms. The QUEEN is a relative of the KAIsEn, he personally likes her, and she is the oldest Sovereign in Europe. Under these circumstances the CZAR cannot object to the KAISER following his example. Without, how- ever, pretending to- know what may or may not be discussed, we may fearlessly say that the meet- ing at Potsdam was of good augury for the maintenance of the peace of Europe. If we could have induced President KRUGIR to visit this country and talk over affairs with our PRIME Minister and other statesmen, we might have adjusted before now all the points in the diplo- matic dispute betweea us, We 11,4ve great faith in these personal conferences, and they contribute to the friendliness which makes for peace and

Cyclists are making a determined effort to secure justice for themselves and their machines at the hands of the Rail way Companies. The trade and the public are united in the statter,and on Tuesday Mr. Herhert Gladstone, M.P., headed an influ- ential deputation to the authorities of the Railway Clearing House. We hope Mr. Glad- stone's eloquence may have a softening effect on the flinty hearts of the zailway companies, but we doubt whether much real benefit will accrue from the efforts of the deputa- tion. There is something about railway magnates- which makes them particularly difficult people to convince, or move from a position they have taken up. They know that their railroads are an absolute necessity to the public, and that no matter how much the latter may fume and fret at inconveniences they have to endure they cannot retaliate by any system of boycott. We have to travel about, and we have to convey our goods from place to place—by rail. Motor cars being in their infancy and flying-machines merely short cuts to destruction, we are entirely dependant on railways managed by gentlemen who look at dividends first and the convenience of their customers last of all. Cyclists contribute a larpe sum to the revenue of the railways, and they do not mind doing so as long as they get a quid pro quo, But when they pay 6d. for 12 miles, 9d. for 25, and is. for 50 miles carriage of their cycles and at the same time know that the valuable mounts have to take " pot-luck " as regards safety in transit, they feel that justice and fair treat- ment are not being meted out to them. Neither do they feel that they are getting full value when 4d. has to be paid for half-an-hour's deposit of the cycle in the cloak-room. If the railway com- panies only prove amenable to reason then the cyclists' cause is won, for there is no combating the arguments in favour of redressing these grievances, but if the companies prove unsympa- thetic and shield themselves behind incomprehen- sible and inadequate Acts of Parliament, as they .have done in the past, then the cyclist will be a much-to-be-pitied individual. We are in hopes, however, that the voioes of hundreds of thousands of wheel-men and wheel-women praying for reform will have the wished for eilect.

IT will be interesting to observe how far the first act of the South African campaign will change current opinion of the British army as an effective fighting force. We have all been nurtured in the belief that victories can always be won by British forces in the face of great odds. In Natal we have had one of those situations wherein the truth or fallacy of this belief could be strikingly ex- emplified. It is painful to acknowledge that we have won no great victories. The plain fact of the situation is that we have had to yield positions mile by mile. We have stemmed the advance to Durban, but we have not broken it. In engagements we have pressed the enemy back, but have not discouraged him or pre- vented him from gaining strategic advantages. Now, whatever may be said, this is not in accord- ance with British traditions. We have not gained any signal and overwhelming victory with a small force against a large one, and this fact,unless such a victory is gained before the Army Corps arrives at the front, must compel us to revise some of our most cherished beliefs. The truth is, the glamour of history and of empire have led us to exaggerate. Our soldiers are biave—none braver. Given equal conditions they will win anywhere. But in a great minority we can no longer count upon victory as our certain heritage ; and when this is recognised there follows a still more tremendous sequel, which must one day soon be seriously faced,unless we are willing to compromise our security as a world Power. The sequel is Conscription; and we think that a signal victory in Natal within the next ten days will postpone it, just as the lack of such a victory, by disillusioning us in reference to our fancied superiority everywhere, will hasten it'.

Each successive report on sewerage questions makes it abundantly evident that the old-fashioned method of purification by precipitation will yield to the bacterial treatment. The danger of the pollution of rivers is avoided by the new scheme, for sanitary experts testify emphatically that the effluent discharged into the river is purer than the water of the river itself. That fact was made ap- parent in the series of lectures delivered before the Society of Arts by Dr. Samuel Rideal last winter, and it is noteworthy that the Manchester Corporation are now being advised to adopt the bacterial system. T!ie doubt at Manchester is,with so much chemical ref use,would the bacteria thrive? That has been set at rest by experts, and it is well for the country they have come to the conclusions they have. It would never do to have it said that, in order to be free from the dangers of microbes, one must live in the most squalid districts of Cotteno- polis, and drink the dark, poisonous-looking water of the river in preference, for example, to the clear sparkling liquid from a country brooklet. Every- one knows that in those apparently innocent streamlets there often lurks an epidemic in posse. With regard to Manchester the conclusion is satis- factory, because there s an increasing tendency to make chemical discharges into our rivers; and if these threatened to nullify new theories of sewage treatment—well, it would be bad for the new theories, and we should have to go back to the old occupying a large quantity of land, and in- voffing expensively-elaborated works.

In the course of its history Warrington has had many traducers, and that is perhaps why a bad name still clings to the ancient town in many quarters. A recently-published book entitled "Echoes of Old Lancashire," contains the impres- sions of a literary lady named Miss E. J. Spence during a visit to the manufacturing districts of Lancashire in 1807. From her writings we learn that the inns of Warrington did not please her,

===== 0002601_18991110_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
and "the dirtiness of the people here exceeds," she says, " what I could have believed in any part of this kingdom." The criticism may or may not have been just. No doubt our ancestors would hotly deny the truth of her description of the people of Warrington in the early years of this rapidly closing century. But Warrincrtou 'las never been an Arcadia and never will. his however, somewhat consoling to know that Warrington did not stand alone under the lash of this severe critic. The following description is not of Warrington but of Manchester district. "I wish I could tell your ladyship," she says, " that the peasantry were possessed of that native simplicity we expect to find two hundred miles from the Metropolis; their manners aecorA with their rude and uncultivated appearance, and their demeanour is remarkably forbidding; but this 1 understand, is often the case near manufacturing towns, though it is the first time I have had the opportunity of observing it." There now! who would have thought that was correct, even ninety years ago.

In the able paper which Mr. A. J. Jolley read at the North-Western Poor Law Conference at Manchester on "The differentiation in the treat- ment of indoor cases," reference was made to the success which has attended the classification scheme, as it has been called, at the Warrington Workhouse. The title of Mr. Jolley's paper may have a forbidding look to anyone who does not take a very deep interest in poor law adnainistra- tion, but Mr. Jolley brought out many facts which the public would do well to become acquainted with. To those unfamiliar with the classification scheme it may be briefly stated that the inmates of the Warrington Workhouse have been classified by character and accommodated in senarate rooms as far as the present premises permit. The best and most deserving paupers are placed in the "A " . class whilst the worst are allocated to class " Little luxuries am o allowed to the " A's" whilst the " D's " are sternly dealt with and their privi- leges greatly curtailed. After a twelve months' trial Mr. Jolley was able to inform the delegates at the Conference that the Warrington Guardians had materially improved the lot of our aged deserv- ing indoor poor, without adding to the burden of the ratepayers, and the stricter treatment of the other inmates had led to a steady decrease in the number of inmates and a consequent diminution of the total cost of maintenance. Warrington was not, of course, the first to adopt this scheme—it seldom is a pioneer in enterprise of this kind—but the results are of sufficient importance to warrant other unions in taking a leaf out of Warrington's book.

Just as in Warrington, there has been all over the country a remarkable decrease of interest in the municipal elections this November. The war has absorbed the attention of everybody, and where the municipal contests have been held the rivalry has only been slight and generally of a per- sonal rather than a political character. In War rington the Liberal and Conservative parties re- main practically uninfluenced by the elections though the Government adherents have some small gain to congratulate themselves upon if they count heads. But we are not dis- cussing the elections from a political point of view. The Council has obtained two valuable additions in Messrs. L. Greening and A. J. Jolley. Both are men of ability, principle, and firmness, and can be relied upon to render good service to those who have placed their trust in them. We are pleased, too, that the retiriag Councillors who sought office again, have been re-elected, and particularly Mr. J. Shaw Green, whose loss would have been almost irreparable had he suc- cumbed to his opponent in Bewsey Ward. Gener- ally speaking the personnel of the Cuuncil has been altered for the better this N w3rniler, and the Council was, perhaps, never more strongly con- stituted.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[By A CORRESPONDENT.' Not once or twice, but rather a dozen times, have I referred to the inefficiency of the Runcorn Fire Brigade. The state of things is really too bad and for a community comprising some 17,000 people with agricultural suburbs of another straggling 10,000 to be without an adequate means of coping with fire is a gross temptacion of providence. Luckily the last few fires have resulted in nothing more serious than the loss of several well-insured baystacks—bad they b-en any of our large works, the result would be just the same, the brigade would have to " watch them burn out." A calamity of this kind, however,would be a disaster that more up-to-date appliances would stand a better chance of preventing—though, indeed, it is often enough the case that fire gets its own way when opposed by the best appliances and men. I wonder how many more object lessons the public will have to have before a move is made.

The Local Government Board Inquiry respecting the borrowing of £4,000 for the purposes of drain- ing, levelling and putting into execution the admirable scheme of Mr. Joseph Wilding (surveyor to the R.U.D.C.) for the conTersion of the Runcorn Heath and Common into a park of acceptable size and prepossessing appearance—is now a thing of the past. For a few days before it came off we moved as it were beneath a heavy cloud of opposition and rumours of opposition. From all sides people predicted the up-rising of the town, and at one stage an organised opposition headed by a full-fiedged barrister was talked of. But what were the real facts : only one man, be it said to his eternal credit, had the courage of his convictions, and that one man was Mr. D. Brshrown. Personally I am in favour of the park;t.ut I do and will honour " Bisbrown" for his staunchness to his principles. Certainly the oppo-3ition he offered was not very great,it was only the Question of whether the town could or could not afford the luxury, and on that question the Local Government Bard will natur- ally satisfy themselves before they proceed to grant their sanction.

With regard to the purchase of a horse ambu- lance, for which £6O was revired, no opposition was offered at all. The confessions of Mr. Lake "that the only vehicle now in use by the Council was a dilapidated old cart, through the bottom of which there were continual fears of the patients falling out" was indeed a revelation of a state of affairs shameful to contemplate. I have known for some time that thins were bad, but not nearly so bad as that. Anyhow the horse ambulance will most likely be an accomplished fact soon, and the only hope is that it will not be required often.

The classes in connection with the St. John Ambulance Association are now commencing and for years a class of males has fburished in Ilanclrn with more or less success. Nothing however is heard of a class tor females. Such classes have been founded with admirable success almost all ov:tr the country, and at them the ladies—who by the way are remarkably deft at bandaging—are taught the various methods of treating and rendering first aid to the injured. In the position of house wife, the ladies have often calls to Infaet an emergency, and often enough have to depend upon customary usage, neighbourly advice, and so on, for their mo-le of procedure, whilst a little elementary knowledge such as that given by the St. John Ambulancl Association would put them in a better position to deal with the injury. Let me hope this suggestion will be considered favourably.

A meeting is to be held in Runcorn for the pur- pose of" raising the wind '' for " the kids that Tommy left behind him." and an opportunity will be given to all to extend tangible—the only genuine —sympathy. May everyone who can carry a Coin attend that meeting and leave it behind when the bat goes round, for a more deserving case can- not be found.

DEATH OF Mn, WILLIAM GITTENS,

There has passed away .by the death of Mr. William Gittens, f Lowland E-road, a townsman wlao was amongst one of the most respected and revered of all. A native of Shropshire he came to Runcorn about the time the L. and NW. Railway Viaduct was being con- stitscted (about 36 years ago) and has since that time lived the life of an honest, straightforward townsman doing that which his hand found to do with all his good will. Ever since his advent in the town he identified himself with the cause of the Primitive Methodists—a cause in which he has accomplished valuable and lasting work,all in an unobstrusive and unostentatious manner. During the storm on Friday afternoon, shortly after one o'c'ock,two large wooden sheds which were in course of erection at Messrs. Gessage's Soap Works were blown down.
