DESTRUCTIVE STACIE- FIRE AT RUNCORN.

About 9.30 on Monday evening two stacks of straw and one of hay, the pronerty of Mr. John Richardson, farmer, Sutton, were discovered to be on fir in the stackyard on Halton-road near to the Recreetion Foot- ball ground. The a!arm sprrad, and with creditable alacrity the Runcorn Fire Brigade were on the scene with the reel cart. C-nsiderable difficulty was, how- ever. experienced in getting, a supply of water adequate enough to cope with the fire, which by this time had asAinved tremendous proportions. Hundreds of people from the "fair ground" upon which the Ruocorn Wakes were being celebrated were attracted to the site of the fire, and the whole scene was one of considerable splen- dour, and when the fire was at its height afforded a magnificent spectac'e.. lighting up the country-side for miles around. The brigade were unable to be of any use in the way of saving the produce, but were able to prevent its spread to two adj icent stacks. They ren- dered valuable assistance and remained on the snot batt- ling the flames until the following afternoon. The dam- age done is estimated at £l5O. It is stated that the fire originated by the sparks from a " rocket " sect up to celebrate the anniversary of the gunpowder plot, alight- ing on the loose straw around the stacks. RAFFLING v. GAMBLING AT RUNCORN. -- Vicar Wood's Sophistry.

We have been beguiled by devious means in the past in believing that the Rev. Canon A. Maitland Wood, M.A. Vicar of Runcorn, was somewhat of a logician. After his ob-ervations in this month's Parish Magazine we are compelled to believe that he is a logician of the Jesuitical order. It is no new dectrine that " the end justifies the means," but that Canon Wood should adopt such a one is surprising to say the least of the matter. Here are his oh-ervatious in their own unblushing garb : It is the fashion to abuse Bazaars, and certainly they are an expensive way o! raising money, but, in a community such as ours, they evoke an amount of co-operation, of friendliness, of self-lertial and generosity on the part of people of very limited means, such as one cannot be too thankful for and such as must be appmved by Him" Wlso loveth a cheerful giver," They lead sometimes to quarrels, true I but so does everything in which numbers of people ars engaged together when those peep e put themselves before the welfare of the whole off iir—and raffles are as bad as gambling—but are raffle I at a Bazaar rightly to be called gambling? I very much doubt it. The evil of gambling lies on the one hand in it's tendency to snake men rids m mey they cannot afford or money not their own : on the other hand it is the outcome of a desire to gain through the toss of another, this gambling is contrary to the principles of responsibility and love. No man of Christian principle can gamble because he is responsible to God for the use of his money and because his neighbours' loss is not a pleasure to him, but a sorrow. When I put into a raffle at a bazaar lam spending what I feel I can and ought to spend, but I spend on the joint stock principle in order to promote the sale of o.rtieles which no one pre3eW, can affsrd to bu 7 and if I win a prize I take nothing from anyone." To the ordinary carnal mind it is clear that Canon Wood has had a war in his camp—that there have been objections to the manner in which the bazaar has been conducted which have led to internecine strife, although the manner in which he refers to the affair is enough to make the shade of Lindley Murray twist his airy form into countless contor- tions. Bazaars, he tells us, sometimes lead to quarrels when people selfishly place themselves be- fore the object to be gained and then he parentheti- cally adds" And raffles are as bad as gambling," as though this was his own sentiment. Toe end of the sentence, however, shows that he is voicing an objection which has been raised at the bazaar, for he asks " but are raffi IS at bazaars rightly to be I called gambling" The sentence is much more in- volved than are the British troops in the present Icampaign in South Africa ; but it is satisfactory to find that the Vicar knows what he intends to con- vey even if his readers may not comprehend. Re replies to his own question, however, in a manner that leaves no room for doubt as to his opinions,but he does it in such a way as to leave no doubt that he is combating and endeavouring to still the "small voice "of his own conscience. That a minister of the " reformed " religion in the latter end of the nineteenth century should dare to print such un- adultered jesuitical sophistry is almost beyond be- lief; and if Canon Wood has thereby stilled the small voice of his conscience, we are sorry for his conscience and still more sorry should we be if the people of Runcorn believed him. Not long ago in a heated discussion at the School Board, Canon Wood said that to argue from the particular to the general was bad logic, and altogether at variance with the true principles of debate. And yet, here, Canon Wood eeeks to maintain his approval of the gambling rafille, by a brief and paltry analysis of his own feelings when he risks a plebian shilling in the hope of receiving in return a five guinea article. Weakly indeed does he argue ! When he puts into a Witte, he spends what he fe3ls he can and ought to spend, but he does it he says on the joint stock principle in order to pronaote the sale of artieles which no one present can afford to bay. Is it the rule then that in an honest joint stock con- cern one shareholder takes the whole of the profits and leaves the rest of the providers of the stock to starve. What would our local co-operatore who are essentially pint stock traders say if their chair- man Ir. Dailey swept the whole of the " divi " into his own pockets? Imagination isltoo feeble to conjure up the commotion there would be in Church-street at the end of the half year. In joint stock concerns the profits are properly apportioned and there is no desire to "gain through the loss of another." The Vicar says if he wins a prize he takes "nothing from anyone. But the Vicar is wrong. In raffling the winner does gain at the loss of the whole of the other con- tributors. If there are 100 shilling contributors to a raffle, 99 are losers of their money without hope of recovery, and the hundredth man chuckles that the turn of Fortune's wheel has placed in his hands an article for which he has by no means paid full value. This is gambling purely and simply, and, therefore, contrary to the principles of responeibi- lity and love. Reffling is as bad or even worse than gambling on a race course. The Bookmaker con- ducts his affairs on bueiness lines. For certain risks he off-bre various prices, and they can be taken or rejected by his clients. Those who are fortunate enough to stake their money on the winning horse receive in proportion to their risk, and the others lose; and it would puzzle the brain of an ordinary man—but perhaps not of a Canon who is Chairman of a School Board—to discover why it is more evil to put a shilling "on a horse "than it is to put it on the number of a raffia ticket.

Does the Vicar deem it necessary to make his illogical remarks because his bazaar had ta depend largely upon the gambling raffi ? ; and does he seek to quiet, his own conscience and to persuade the losers of their hard earned money into the belief that that which they gambled was in reality a gift to the Lord. "Gambling," says the learned Canon, tend "to make men risk money they cannot afford or money not their own" but " raffles" at bazaars tend to "self-denial and generosity on the part of people of very limited means." Otherwise gambling for your own gain is evil but gambling for your own gain, with the financial benefit of the church also in view, is good, altimugh it, may some- times lead to quarrels. If the Vicar can impose up on the people of Runcorn with such wishy-washy- sophistry as this no wonder he and his party cap- tured the School Board at the last election with "figure guzzling."—NEMESlS.

RUNCORN FREE PUBLIC LIBRARY.

Free Lectures for the Winter at the Technical Institute.

The first of a series of free lectures, to be delivered in connection with the Runcorn Free Public Library and Museum—(for since the Chaowick bequest it can lay claim to the addition)—illustrative of the treasures therein, was given last Monday evening at the Runcorn Tecunical Institute, where a fair attendance had gathered in the New Wing, to hear Mr. Robert Newstead (fellow of the Etomological Society of London, Curator of the Grosveeor Museum, Chester,) lecture on "British birds : their habits and wanderings." The chair was occupied by Mr. F, Wiswall (chairman of the Runcorn Urban Dis- trict Council), and there were also prest nt:—Messrs. W. Handley (chairmen of the Library CornmiVee), D. Bis- brown, G. Hewitt, F. J. Moulton, J. D. Jones (librarian), S. Pope, F. Hutton, Rev. W. Scholes, etc.— Mr. F. Wiswall, in introducing the Lecturer, said he did so with great pleasure. as Mr. Newstead bad so kindly consented to come and deliver the first Iccture for them. As they had seen the title of the lecture was " British Birds: their habits and wanderings," but he could assure ttem the only experience he had had of British or any other birds was on one occasion when he went bird- nesting as a boy. On that occasion, however, he got so thoroughly disgusted with himself for taking so many ggs that be never went again. After living for a long time in Manchester away from the sight and sound of the country, and the birds, he Gould say one could hardly express the delight and pleasure with which he went out into the country of a morning and heard the thrush and the lark.—Mr. Robert Newstead then delivered an able lecture. Comprehensively em- bracing his subint he divided it into three heads (1) the Spring or Summer migratory, (2) the so-called resident birds, wad (3) the Autumn end Winter migra- tories. Aided with limelight views (thrown on the screen by a powerful lantern operated by Mr. Sydney Buckley) the lecturer lucidly explained the habits and wanderings of the many groups of birds contained under his three heads of discourse. The object of migration was for the purposes of obtaining food. During the summer months, as they were all aware, the atmosphere was filled with insects and the birds had but to, open their mouths to have them filled, but if they stayed in the Northern Countries during the winter and frost they would most likely die of hunger. At the conclusion of an interesting and instructive address Mr. Newstead expressed the h )pe that his remarks naigiet be the moans ot creating more interest in the beautiful collection of ornithological specimens which the Runcorn library contained, many , of which were among tee finest he had ever seen. It only remained for them to be classified and named, and he felt sure they would become a means of education to the people.—Mr. Wiswall preposed, and Mr. Handley seconded a healtv vote of thanks to the lecturer, and to Mr. Sydney Buckley, the lantern operator. —Mr. New- stead respon&d, and on his motion Mr. Wiswall was accorded a like honour, whice was duly acknowledged. —The next lecture will be given by the Rev. C. W. L. Christien on "My friends on the Shelf," on the 20th of November."

THE HORSE AMBULANCE.

Mr. F. A. Lilts explained that the Urban District Council required £6O for the purpose of purchasing a horse ambulance. The infectious diseases hospital was situate about one mile and an eighth from the town. The present mode of conveying the patients there was with an old dilapidated cab, not fit for use, and they were afraid that the patients would fall through the bottom. It was proposed to pur- chase the new vehicle from the British Waggon Company at a cost of £6O, delivered. The Surveyor (Mr. James Wilding), said the vehicle would be one of the ordinary one horse type in which a patient could be laid at full length. It would be constructed of oak and ash. He handed in a photograph of the vehicle. There was no oppatition. THE PARIC.—MR. BISBROWN OBJECTS.

THE POOR L&W CONFERENCE

Messrs. Alfred R Norman, George Pilling, William Bradford, Thomas Wright, and the Clerk, who attended as delegates the North Western Poor Law Con- ference, held at Manchester on the 27ch and 28th of October, reported :—The president (sir John T. Bibbed) in his opening address gave very instructive statistics relating to the relief of he poor, and very interesting particulars of the gradual improve- ment in the administration of the Poor Laws as practiled by the various Boards of Guardians throughout the country.—Mr. Samuel T. Lunt, chairman of the West Derby Board of Guardians, read a paper upon "The creatment of Tuberculosis by Poor Law Guadiana." After giving statistics showing that the disease was steadily decreasing in this country, he strongly advo- cated the open air treatment and the erection of sana- toria by groups of Boards throughout the country. In the disausaion upon the paper it was very strongly brought out that the insanitary condition of dwellings and the crowded areas in sem) of our large towns were vary largely responsible for the spread of consumption and that plenty of fresh air in dwellings with lofty and large rooms would very greatly assist in stamping out the disease.—Mr. A. J. Jelley, chairman of the Warring- ton Board of Guardians real a very good paper upon the "Differentiation in the treat leant of indoor cases." Fie referred to the diff-rentiation as now practised in several unions throughout the country and strongly advocated the advisability of treating the deserving poor in their old age with more leniency and kindness than those who become inmates of the Workhouse through their own fault. There was a good discussion upon the paper and there was a very stro-,g feeling in favour of Mr. Joller's contention.—On the Saturday a very in- teresting paper upon "OH Age Pensions" was read by Mr: Hooke, of Manchester, a strong feeling being ex- pressed by the conference in favour of some system of "Old age pension," but it was pretty generally felt that it would be difficult to take a hard and fast line suitable to the whole counzry. Mr. Meadewcroft asked wh,ther copies of the report of the conference could be got in extenso ? The Chairman replied he believe they could at a cost of I s . each.

n*Mr. Pillineprorwed, and Mr. A. R. Norman seconded, th it such reports b 3 ottainced and circulated amongst the mPna hers.

Mr. Meadowcroft added it was corta'nly desirable that the Board should have the reports so as to obtain the necessary information ; indeed, it would be of ad- vantage t 3 the union and they mieht not lose all the expenses of the delegates to the conference for nothing. He supplrted the resolution which was carried.

ST. HELENS.

FATAL ACCIDENT IN A ST. HELENS COLLIERY.

On Wednesday, Mr. S. Briehou3e. county coroner, held an inquest at St. Helens Down Hall on the body of Edward James William, 32 years of age, collier, of 52, Orrell-street, who was killed at Ashton's Green Colliery, on Tuesday morning. From the evidence it appeared that while Williams was at work a large portion of the roof collapsed, and two big stones dropped upon him. He was knocked down, an I was so badly crushed that when his body was releas A it was found that life was extinct. Mr. 11. Hall, the Government Inspector of Mines, considered that the affair was purelv an accident, and the jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death."

Presentation to Mr. J. E. Warren.

After the Lincashire v. Yorkshire match the teams and committees dined together at the White Swan Hotel, Halifax, Mr. H. If. Waller, president of the Yorkshire County Committee, in the chlir. The usual loyal toast having been honoured, an interes- ting presentation was made to Mr J. E. Warren, of Warrington, a past president of the Union, and until recently a member of the committee, in ap- preciation of his valuable services during the past four years. The presentation which consisted of a very handsome gold English lever watch was made on behalf of the members of the Northern Union Rugby Football Com- mittee by Mr. Waller, in the absence of Mr. J. H. ;inith, of Widnes, the president. After explaining that Mr. Smith had intended to be present at the match, and had actually started, but was taken ill suddenly at Rochdale Station and had to be sent back home, Mr. Waller spoke in the highest terms of Mr. Warren's services to the Northern Union. No man had fought harder that Mr. Warren for the success of the Union, and for the carrying wit of its principles. In his reply Mr. Warren said one man would not be missed by the Northern Union now. (" Question.") That body was on too solid a basis, and too firmly estab- lished. He wished the Union every success, and hoped he would always retain the friendship of the members of the committee. The watch is adorned on the back with a design, the basis of which is a white rose,the centre of the flower is occupied with three shields, indicating the arms of Yorkshire, Lancashire and Cheshire, and reflects the greatest credit upon the artistic taste of Messrs. Pattorini and Sons, of Bradford, who supplied the beautiful gift.

A WARRINGTON CARTER SENT TO GAOL.

At the Warrington Police Court on Friday, before the Mayor and other magistrates. John Baxter, a carter, re- siding in Tanner-'ane, was charged with stealing four cart loads of gravel value Gs the property of alward Isherwood, some time between Tuesday last and the previous day.—The Chief Constable said the man was employed as a carter by Mr. Ei ward Isherwood who hada contract to supply gravel for the new asylum at Winwick. It was the man's duty to cart the gravel from Richardson's pit Walton, to the Asylum. On Thursday m -nning, Mr. Isherwood being suspicious gave the man instructions to cart some manure to Acton Grange, and afterwards take some gravel to the Asylum. Ho should have returned at three o'clock in the afternoon and accounted for his time. Be did not come back till 8 o'clock under the influence of drink. Mr. Isherwood questioned the man more closely and he owned us to stealing the gravel, saying he had taken it to a Mr. Worsloy who was building in Forstrir-street and bad got a shilling for it. Inquiries were made and it was found that the gravel had b!en sold to Mr. Worley for two shillings. Be had taken two cart loads on Tuesday one on Wednesday, and one on Thursday. When charged he said " I don't call it stealing." It appeared that Mr. Worsley questioned the man, and he said that be had to take clinkers to a place and got the gravel in exchange for R.—Prisoner was sent t) gaol for one menth with hard labour. Latchford Improvement Society —The Transvaal War.

A. meeting of this s.)ciety wished in the Tem- perance Hall on Friday evening, there being a good attendance. Two persona were nominated for elec- tion. Mr Walter Hill read a paper on the " Trans- vial War : is it righteous ?" Tho essayist said the action of our own Government was unwise, and not very politic. It hastened the ultimatum of the Boers, and along with it war and death. Several speakers combated the essayist's views, and the tendency of thought was that though war was a calamity we had to see this dispute milt, and a settlement made on a firm foundation. Justice and freedom were involved, and the privileges and rights of all blacks and whites were desired and deman- ded. In 1890 public revenue was paid in the fol- lowing proportions :—By blacks 3 per cent, h y Boe a 71 per cent., and by British 89i per cent. The expenditure for objects both Boer and British alike £1,183,273, but the Boer expenditure and non- British and wholly Boer was £515,491. Let any impartial mind consider these items, as in them there was a world of meaning. No doubt South Africa was an intere,stinz problem, capable of ex- pansion, but after all there were interests at stake beyond big diamonds and big dividends.

"A SWINDLE."

This P.era has been g in the mu ach of the Australian press :—" A. Swindle" is the name that appears over the door of a struggling lawyer in an upTe)antry town in New South Wales. Death of Mrs. J. Malin Ede!sten.

It is with regret that we record the death of Mr s J. Malin Edmiston, of Quer:Acid, Latchford, which took place on Saturday,in Lonlvn,after undergoing a severe operation. The deceased lady was deeply respected in Warrington and district, and she will be much missed by a large cirAe of friends with whom she had made herself very popular. She leaves a husband and two sons to mourn her loss The two latter are Dr. Ernest Edeisten, of London and Captain J.A. Edelsten, of Warrington. Loyal Orange Institution, Annual Ball.

The annual ball in connection with the local branch of the Loyal Orange Institution of England was held in the Assembly Room of the Conservative Club on Monday evening. The arrangements had been admirably carried out by a committee consist- ing of _Messrs. W. Bloomfield, DM., M Edwards, J Wright, A Bloomfield and W J Bloomfiel4 (secre- tary) and a most enjoyable evening was spent. About sixty couples put in an appearance and dance music was supplied by Mr. Alfred Wright's band_ The catering, which was in the hurls of Mr. Wat kins, manager of the Club gave great satisfaction. Mr. J 1 Bloomfield officiated as M.C. The proceeds were in aid of the Benevolent fund.

THIRD RALLY.

Band of Hope Children in Parr Hall. There was again a large gathering in Parr Hall on Thursday last week, when the third united rally in con- nection with the Warrington Band of Hope Union was field. The president of the Union (Mr. R. A. Naylor) occupied the chair, and he was supported on the plat- form by Councillor Ellison. Rev W Bracecamp, Rev J S Hughes, and Mr E Lloyd Jones (secretary). The Arch- deacon of Warrington (the Ven Archdeacon Madden, M.A.) arrived at a later stage of the proceedings and delivered an address. The Band of Hope Union Choir was also accommodated on the platform, and under the conductorship of Mr Walter Brough swig several choruses in a highly creditable manner. Mr E Wilkes, A.L.C.M., presided at the organ. The soloist for the evening was Mr John Henry, R.A:M. whe sang" Why do the Nations Rage " (Handel) and "Guiding Light." The music of the latter song was the artiste's own composition, and Mr Henry scored a distinct success. An interesting item was the dialogue "Mrs Maggs is caught" by members of the Stockton Heath Band of Hope. The characters were taken by the following : Mr Maggs, Mr George Moss ; Mrs Maggs, Miss Sarah Jane Worrall ; Billy, Master Bonn Moss • Mr .Kidney (the butcher), Mr John Worrall • Mr Sponge,(the baker), Master Robert T Jones; Mt Chall(the milkman), Master Bertie Hughes ;Mr Fatface (the publican), Mr Bradford Kenworthy ; and the pliceman, Me Septimus Moss. A recitation entitled " I mean to be a man" was also given by Master G Whitfield. The CHAIRMAN at the outset complitneuted the choir • upon their excellent singing and paid a tribute to the services of Mr. Brough as conductor and Mr. Wilkes as organist. He felt sure they could not devote their energies, time and means for the advancement of any. movement that needed assistance more, and was calcu- lated to do more good than the movement in connection with the Band of Hope Union. (Hear, hear.) The March they had just sung had quite a martial air about it. Everyone knew they were eugagrd in a terrible I war. That was not the occasion to expres3 any opinion on that war, but every true I patriot must hope that the army of the Queen would succeed and come off victeritus. (Applause.) He was only talking to a man in the town that day who might be called up for active service. He had fought in the Soudan campaign, and he told him (Mr. Naylor), that he would not hesitate to go to-morrow to join his company and fight for his country. (Applause.) That was a noble spirit, but they wanted a lot of fight- ing at home. Ho believed it was the late Duke of Albany who eaid that this country had only one enemy to fear, and that wsa strong drink. (Applause.) The temperance question needed to be considered just as that great war. What was drink doing in this country ? It was desolating houses, robbing little children of their right, and sending thousands every year to a drunk.ird's grave. They in the temperance movement believed they were right on this question and did not intend to give in. (Applause.), Archdeacon MADDEN, in the course of an earnest ad- . tress, said it was a sad fact that when people gave way to drink they seemed to lose all sense of truthfulness. He had been told lies by the hundred for the sake of trying to" wheedle "him out of fourpenee or sixpence for drink. He based his objectien to the drink 'traffic on the ground that every public-house was more or less a source of moral degrade.- datioire wherever it was placed. That was a string assertion and be realised the seriousness of it. Did they ever hear of a philanthnpist giving money hr the erection of a public-house for the benefit of the com- munity ? (Hear, hear.) It was for all of them to ask themselves what was their duty in the face ot such a trafficwhich was sapping and undermining the welfare of the community, Unless they had faced this goes •tion as intelligent men ad women they lid not risen

to their duty as citizens and as beings worthy of taking I their place in the future of this great country. (Ap- plause.) He had no sympathy for those who sat on the hedge in regard to this question. It was useless of people crying out for the Government to do something; the Government would not move in the matter until the.people had made up their minds as to what they wanted. It was for the working men of this country to make their voice heard on this question, but they would not do so until a public opinion bad been created amongst them. At the present time working- men were the best customers t 3 the publicans, and a great work needed doing in getting the working-classes on the side of Temperance. This could only be done by every person who believed in temperance using his or bee influence in the sphere in which they lived and worked. (Applause.) The services of the speakers and singers having been acknowledged, a successful meeting terminated with the singing of the National Anthem.

CHANCELLOR ESPIN AT

WARRINGTON.

The anniversary service in connection with the ) Warrington Church Society was held in St. Paul's Church on Thursday last week. There was a fai congregation, the preacher being the Worshipful Chancellor Espin, D.D. The service was intoned by the Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth, and appropri- ate hymns were sung. Chancellor Espin preached an excellent sermon, taking for his text Timothy 3, 15, "The House of God, which is the Church of the Living God, the pillar and the ground of the truth." He said that the Church Society was by its name an association of a raligious character and unless it in some way represented the church and did something to promote church principles it was false to its title. Just as a mechanic's ' institute was designed for the advancement of Imechanics, so was the church society for the advance- ment of the welfare of that great religious union, the I church. What was the church ? In the first place by the church they did not mean the established church. They valued its position as a national institution, and they meant to keep that position, which he thoeght they could do. The established church was an anvil that had worn out many hammers, and he suspected that it was going to wear out more hammers yet. The church existed ages before England became a nation, and it was more correct to say that the church created the state than the state created the church. Had it not been for the church there would not have been an English nation at all. The preacher observed that he had seen correspondence in " The Times " respecting the propor- tion of Romanists in the country. Is was stated that eight thousand converts were made each year. If they considered they would find that that was somethi g like seven henared in each month. The population of hose Weeds was increasing at the rate of about twenty three thousand each month, so that it was nothing very much after all. Nothing was said of the numbers who left the church of Rome to come to the Church of England. The bulk of the converts to Roman- ism were not from the Church of England but he was told from the Presbyterian church. The Church of Eng- land,however,should attempt an enrichment of it forms of -worship. They must have new services,a second even- song and a form for the burial of infants, amongst other things. The church in America had accom- plished both of these and he thought that if the American church could do it they could also. It was one of the duties of their society to bring forward these church queEtions and carefully discuss them, and if possible pass resolutions respecting them. The preacher concluded by impressing upon the congregation the work that the Church Society should do and asked the members to set an example by thei own personal habits—A etAlection was taken in aid or ,the funds of the .S.OC'E ty.

The Municipal Elections. Sir,—We have been told that it is better for munici- pal elections not to be politically contested, but it seems to me that a union of the two poltical parties is far more undesirabV, for at present. it practically places in the hands of a few, the results of any contests, and as an individual ratepayer I 4onsider it my duty to express disapproval. Though the two gentlemen who have been returned in place of the two retiring Councillors. are eminently fitted for that position I regret the circumstances under which they were elected. VV•hat is the meaning of this change ? Is there anywhere a desire to keep out of the Council men who strongly disapprove of methods adopted during the last twelve months. Is it possible that such an influence May have helped to bring about a union of political parties. The election of c,Duncillors ought not to be left in the hands of a few, for if so it might possibly fall into the hands of an interested few. It ought to be left in the hands of the ratepayers unbiassed by selfish con- siderations or political views. Intimidation bribery and corruption are penal offences because they seduce the ratepayers and preve,t them returning to Parliament the met who would otherwise be elected. I do not say that anyone in Warrington would be guilty of bribery, but refer to it to show how very undesirable it would be to call into force any union of interests that might have a similar effect. The methods adopted in public life are of very great importance, and I am not sure that the methods of conducting public business in Warrington are yet quite perfect. I do not wish to be offensive, and hones ly wish that I could write a letter that would he pleasant all round, but every rate- payer as well as every soldier ought to do hi 3 duty, for the welfare of the public is sacred, though sometimes there are few who defend the right.

The right, to which we should be true For which we should for ever care, Of all the subjects we pursue There's none that can with it compare.

They who disregard it to keep or to obtaln a position for themselvo, hinder the progress of mankind and trample on all that is good.

S. KENYON.

Warrington, November 7th, 1899.

The Political Millennium in Warrington.

Sir,—&t the laving of the foundation stone of the new Police Buildings and subsequent proceedings there was a great gathering of the political clans, and so harmonious and enthusiastic were the proceedings that one in- stinctively thought of the promised time when the wolf and the lamb shall lie down together in green pastures. Mr. Councillor John Crosfield seemed to have been fairly carried off his legs as he surveyed the merry and fraternizing Conservatives and Liberals around him. Altogether, the scene was so full of happy omens for the future to all but the unfortunate ratepayers that he cried out that the Millennium had arrived in Warrington at lat. But in the blissful excitement of the moment the worthy Councillor seemel to have forgotten al about the unique incidents whieh led up to the reign of peace. Det me respectfully refresh his memory on the important subject.

When Mr. Wright's tenon of office was nearing a ciose the Conservative majority in ths Council did not manifest any desire that Mr. Alderman Roberts should rule over them. On the contrary, finding that they had not amongst themselves material out of which to manu- facture a worthy successor to Mr. Wright they went outside the Council altogether and made overtures to Mr. Parr in the hope that he might be induced to preside over and adorn their deliberations next year, but Mr. Parr declined the honour with thanks. Whereupon another caucus meet- ing was held, and the few discomfited Conservatives pressnt generously permitted their Liberal friends to gratify their long cherished desire b 7 the nomination of Mr. Alderman Roberts. And, of cours), the worthy Alderman returned thanks for the high honour thus con- ferred upon him. I leave your readers to form their own conclusions as to which of the political parties showed the least d.gnity and sil respect in these trans- actions.

Brit another matter connected with the groat political coalition drserves a few words. When the political leaguers had, or thought they had, made things com- fortable all round by the nomination of Mr. Roberts, with a valour worthy of the occasion they determined, if po sible, to partially disfranchise the Borough tor a couple of years This bold and tyranirc-Ai stroke of policy was evidently int encl-d to p7event intriliers from getting into the dove-cotrs to disturb the peace, and defeat the °tient of the patriotic alliance. It wr-uld be difficult to find suitable terms to denounce this g,ro,s outrage upon the electors of Warrington. President Kruger has been severely blamed for withholding c:t z ship from the mone granbers, gamblers, and orner blacklegs who have swarrned hit° his country in search of gold. But so far as 1 know he tas n.:,t yet been charged with cheating his subjects cf the franchise. But here we have in Warrington the dr-creditable spectacle of liberty boasting Tories and Radicals joining band in hand in a braz attempt to deprive independent electrirrs of the right to chose an vote for can tidates after their own mind. Tory and Radical voters will be pertnitted,under the In.w regime, to vote for their repective colours as usual at the beck of the political wirepullers. But in- dependence at the polls must be stamped out. That the state of th'n;s I have described is possible in a large and important town like Warrington is greatly to be deplored, but it is not yet beyond remedy. If political partyism were driven out of the Town Council, as assuredly it ought to be, a better and healthier state of feeling would prevail, and the Government of the town would no longer .131 left in the hands of a dual- headed political clique.—Youra truly, 4th November, 1899.

lONA.

Soldiers' and Sailors' Families

Association, Warrington Division:

Sir,—! beg to appeal to the genaroeity of your readers on behalf of the Warrington Division of the Soldiers' and Sailors' Families Association. This Society, which is under the patronage of Her Majesty the Queen,is strictly undenomilsational. and is intended to succour the wives and families of soldiers and sailors who are for any cause left in reduced circumstances. Many men from Warrington being at present at the front in South Africa, it is feared that some of those they hAve left behind may have to submit to privations in addition to their anxiety, unless their fellow townsmen remember that "Charity begins at home," and contribute to this local division. Any funds subscribed will be applied to reliPve those in need in Warrington, and I may draw your attention to the fact that the Secretary of State for War and Commander-in-Chief have written to the papers recommending the local divisions of this Society to the generosity of the public, as being in a better position than any central one to ascertain the genuineness of eaci3 case and to relicve it promptly. This Association not only helps the wives and families of m•-,n married with leave, but also those " off the strength " whose cases are often the hardest. Subscriptions will be gladly received by the Hon. Treasurer. George Dakin, Esq., Arlington House, Latchford.—Your faithfully, CLARE MOLYNEUX SEEL, (Vice-President of the Association). The Old Warns, Warrington. November 2nd, 1599.

The Lighting of Stockton Heath.

Sir,—As the above village is growing at a rapid rate, and as the difficultit s of sewage, lighting, etc. ,are there- by increasing, it might be as well to call attention to a power which at present is wasted and which Tight be ntilsed for the permanent improvement of our lightin.t, for which we are paying a heavy price. I refer to the waters of Lumb Brook and the Mill Pool, the I.)rce of which was formerly used for a mill close by. This might be used as the motor for the electric lighting of the village. Water power, with properly constructed turbines, is the best motive power for running dynamos, and at the Mill Pool we have a constant supply. More- over the Ackers Pit would serve as a suitable rsserve supply, either as it is, or with a higher baA. f should be united, too, that a generating station by the Mill Pool would be situated not more than 500 yards from the centre of the village, thus avciding an abnormal length of Tam cable. The time is now ripe for discussing such projects ;as Stockton Heath is, progressively speaking, in its infancy, and land and property cin now be acquired at a more reasonable figure than in the future. The grea: water force at present wasted certainly might brier; about, if properly utili.ed, a considerable saving of rates in later year., rot to mention the convenience of the villagers generally.—l am sir, yours truly,

A;FITON EIAYES. H,ath Bank, Stockton Fl,•ath, 2/11/99.

SHOCKING TRAGEDY.

Quarrel Between Two Warrington Men. VERDICT OF MANSLAUGHTER.

The district known as Howley in Warrington has I this week been thrown into a state of excitement ' in consequence of a tragedy which was enacted in its midst late on Saturday night. The scene of the occurrence was at 40, Wellington-street where two families lived together in the same house. James Poole, his wife and two children—a sou and a daughter—formed on.e family, and the other occu- pants of the house were Thomas Kinsey, an un- married man about 40 years of age and his mother, an aged woman ever 80 years of age, who is bed- fast. Poole was a wireweaver by trade but he had worked very irregularly for some time. Kinsey was a factory operative and he too had not been in regular employment. tin Saturday, beer seems to have been pretty freely consumed in the house, and at night there was a quarrel between Kinsey and Poole about some remarks Kinsey had given utterance to concerning Poole's wife. Mrs. Poole appears to have gone be- tween the two patties, and afterwards she repaired to bed, thinking that if she went away the quarrel would quieten down,and she says she heard nothing more. Shortly before eleven o'clock Poole's son, a young fellow of eighteen, returned to the house, which wai then in darkness. He entered the front room, and on obtaining a light was horrifieo to find his father lying beside the sofa with two terrible wounds on the top of his head and two other frightful wounds on the face. He lying in a pool of blood, and was unconscious. He was conveyed to the infirmary, and died there at 7.20 on Monday evening. The police searched the apartment in which the assault had been committed, and found by the sofa head a poker smeared with blood. Kin- sey had disappeared, but early on Sunday morning the police tracked him to a house in Factory yard, where he had asked permission to stop the night. He was told by Inspector Taylor what. a serious condition Poole was in,and he replied, "I remember nowt about it. We have oftea had rows, and we have al ways made it up in the morning. I don't remember using the poker." He was then taken into custody. On Monday morning, Kinsey was placed in the dock 91,1 charged with inflicting grievous bodily harm upon Poole by striking him on the head with a poker. The Chief Constable then intimatei to the Bench that faint hopes were entertained of Poole's recovery, and he asked for a remand until the following Thursday. The demeanour of the prisoner was commented upon by all who were present in court. He did not betray the slightest emotion, and showed no signs that he realised the gravity of his position. His general appearance is somewhat unusual. There is hardly a hair to be seen on his head or face. He is short in stature and light complexioned, He was dressed in his working clothes and clutched a black cap whilst standing in the dock. The magistrates re- manded him. until Thursday, but before many hours had passed Poole died at the Infirmary; and the position of Kinsey became much mere serious. The same evening Kinsey was informed in his cell of the death of Poo!e. He received the news with great coolness, and was heard whistling the next morning. _ _

A further stage in the proceedings was reached on Wednesday evening when an inquest was held on the body of Poole. The prisoner was accommo- dated with a seat in the dock, and there was less callousness in his demeanour. He evinced the greatest interest in the evidence, and the production of the blood-stained sofa cover attracted his attention. The evidence of Mrs. Poole did not satisfy the Coroner or the jury, and at the close she was censured, the jury expressing the opinion that her evidence was not reliable. She failed to con- vince the court that she neither heard nor saw any- thing of the tragic affair although she was upstairs at the time. Sha denied that she was not sober but admitted that she had had several glass of beer during the day. The jury took a merciful view of the case and returned a verdict of manslaughter against the prisoner who will have to answer that charge at the forthcoming Liverpool Assizes. He will again appear before tbe magistrates on Thurs- day next.

The Inquest and Verdict. A Witness Censured.

The inquest was held at the Pollea Court on Wednes- day evening, b; Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner. Mr. T. Birtles was foreman of the jury. The prisoner Kinsey occudied a seat in the dock. Prior to the inquest the jury viewed the body and visited the scene of the tragedy. Ton Coroner in opening the proceedings said it was a matter of minor importance but he did not know how long the inquiry was going to take. He proposed that the jury should rise at 8-30 and if the ir- quiry was not over then to adjourn it till 10 o'clock on Saturday morning. He thouAt the county could not claim longer services from them. Ho wished to point out one or two matters that would enable them to arrive at a conclusion quickly. When one man was charged with causing the death of another, the jury's position was practically that of a presiding justice. They had not to pass sentence or say whatl-punishment sbou:d follow. When they :had heard the evidence they had tosay "murder or manslaughter." It did not mean that they said it so that Kinsey could receive punishment. Dr. Harold Thorpe, junior houce surgeon at the War- rington Infirmary, said the deceased was admitted te the institution at 12.15 on Saturday night, suffering from injuries. He was under the care of the staff till his death. Since death, assisted by Dr. Smith, he had made an examination. Externally he found two cuts on the top of the head. One of them was at the back of the head at an angle of forty-flee degrees from the medium line of the head on the right side. The cut was two and a half inches long, and went down to the bone laying the bone bare. It was a contused wound done by a broad surface. The other cut was two inches behind at right angles to the medium line. It was one and a half inches long and went down to the bone. It was also a contused wound. It was not pos- sible for both cuts to be inflicted at the same time. Over the chin there were two cuts. They were very deep but so far as he could see they did not go to the bone. They went into the soft portion of the chin. They were very contused especially the upper one. They were about an inch apart and an inch long. It was possible that they could have been received both at the same time. There was a bruise on the right side of the face extending from the corner of the eye to the ear. On admission to the Infirmary blood was coming from the right ear, and there were traces of blood in the ear at the post-mortem examination. There was a large contused bruise at the back of the left hand and a super- ficial ebrssion cut, an inch lung. Internally there was blood in the skull caused by bruising ever the right side of the head. There was a fracture of the skull com- mencing between the two cuts and running in circular form, nearly meeting again at the b Lek. The cause of death was hcemorrhage pressing on the brain. There was a clot of blood about the size of an orange in the skull. If he were struck an the head with a poker that would account for everything be saw. From the breadth of the poker the chin wounds could have been done with one blow.

By the Chief Constable; The injuries would be caused by heavy blows. Conaidlrable force would not have to be used with that poker. He had no idea which injuries were likely to have been inflicted first. It was possible that after his skull bad bean fractured he could have moved again and received further injuries.

Br the Coroner : If the man was standing in the kitchen and Kinsey struck him on the head and walked out he would be able to go to the sofa and lie down, before the bcemorrhage became so great as to render him unconscious.

Dr Reginald Smith, police surze)n, said be assisted Dr. Thorpe to make the pest mortern examination. He agreed with his acoount of the injuries, and with what he said was the cause of death. It was very impro- bable he could have received the injuries except at the hands of someone else. He did not think he could have fallen against anvthing and received the injuries unless he fell fiDm a considerable height.

Mr. Browne did not wish to call any witnesses or to put the prisoner in the box. The Coroner, in summing up. said that the jury might find soes difficanty in arriving et a conclueloa. It seEmed to tem to lo At :at it from a Pgal and logical point of view that they could very sola arrive at a con- clusion in the matter First of all they had to ask themselves " how did the man cline to his death. Did he come te his death from injuries received at the hands of another ?" When they considered that they must take into their minds the fact that there was a quarrel on the night ia question and that there was clear proof that the man was struck by Kinsey. There was also clear prof that the man had two cuts nearly on the top of his head, bruising on the right side of the face, and a fracture of the skull which started between the two cuts and running in circular form till it nearly met again. It was very improbable that the two cuts could have been received in any other way than at ill hands of another, as it ! was impossible for him to fall from a great height in that house, The first qaestion was one of an easy descrip- tion that ought not to give any trouble. If they made up their minds on that point the question was could the person be anybody else other than Kinsey. They had not heard it suggest( d that any person had anything to do with Poole except Kinsey. The evidence showed that Kinsey and Paolo had had fights before on that night, Mrs. Poole saw Kinsey strike her husband. Aiterwarde Kinsey went out of the house and slept at the house of a neighbour. He thought they wouli be satisfied on the point that Kinsey inflicted the injury. If they thought that Poole died from injuries received and that Kinsey inflicted them, th.in Kinsey had unlawfully killed Poole. Then the point that would give the most trouble of all was as to the circumstances under which he killed him. The law said that when one person unlawfully killed another the per- son who killed was guilty of murder unless ercurn- stances proved to the contrary. If the evidence showed ' circumstances of justification, extenuation, or excuse, that would reduce the charge of unlawfully killing or murder to manslaughter. They must bear in mind that Mrs. Poole said distinctly that although her husband was struck with the poker at a certain time in the evening she left him when she want to bd appps.rently in geed health, she left them pacified and there was no trace o f blood at that time. She wanted them to believe—he had a strong suspicion that she could tell them more than he hal done—that when she left the room and went upstai:s, she was under tke impression that her husband was not seriously injured and thought that the quarrel had ceased. If that was s,,no injuries had then b len indicted by Kinsey to cause the deatb,it followed that they must have been infl c7ed :afterwards when no one was there to see how thee were done. The question was, did he inflict the injuries under such circumstances that he did it wilfully and with intent to cause grievous bodily harm.

The jury retirel to consider their verdict, and on their return ten minutes later the foreman handed to the Coroner a written statement to the effect that they were unanimous in saying that Poole died fr am injuries re- ceived at tOe hands of Kinsec, but under such circum- stances as to justify them in saying that it was only manslaughter. Mrs. Poole was then re7,4lled and told by the Coroner that the jury were of the opinion that she had not given reliable evidence. He agrced with their opinion. Mrs. Poole : I have given it as near as I nan, and I can't give any more. The C-Jroner : The jury are men of the world and can judge. The verdict of" Manslaughter" seem,d to c-ime aq a relief to the prisoner, who was removed to the cells below.

A LADYSMITH DESERTER SURRENDERS. Committal to the Warrington Depot. Quite a sensation was created on Monday morning in the Fourth Police Court at Liverpool, over which Mr. T. E Sampson was presiding, by the story that was rela- ted when a young man nem ed John Simpson, of mili- tary appearance, appeared in the dock. It transpired that Simpson, who is about 27 years of age, and belongs to Fleetwood, called at the Chief Detective Office on Sunday morning, and surrendered himself as a deserter from the let Battalion of the Liverpool 11,igi- ment at Ladysmith, Natal. He told P.C. Mullins that owing to some trouble with an officer of the company in which he eves a private, he deserted from his corps on the 10th Sei.gember,tramped close upon 200 miles to Darba,n, disposing of his uniform en the way to the Saffire in exceanee for civilian attire. Ar- rived eventually at the seaport, he secured a berth on a homeward bound ship and worked his passage to England. On referring to the " Police Gazette " the pence officials found recorded there a description of a Private John Simpson, who had deserted from the Ist Liverpool Regiment at Ladysmith. This description tallied closely with the individual who placed himself in their hands. Ho was accordingly detained and taken before Mr. Sampson as detailed above. Tne magis- trates ordered Simpson'e further detention to await a military escort from the depot of nis regiment at War- rington, whither be would then be removed to be dealt with according to the army regulations. In making this order Mr. Sampeon remarked to Simpson that he ought now to be out in South Africi thrashing the Boers, To this Simpson replied promptly that that was the reason he had given himself up to the authorities.

THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS. Details of the Ladysmith Fighting. Fearful Effects of British Swordsmanship.

Durban, Sunlay (delayed in transmission).—Accounts given by native eye-witneases of the fighting near Lady- smith last week are confirmed from other sources. The Kaffirs who Fa w the engagement say that b )th on Friday and Saturday the number of Boers killed and w3unded was terrific. Tee Br i shrikd far mercy. The natives say they never saw or heard of such killing and cutting. The battlefield was covered with bodies. They add that the men in petticoats, evidently the Gor dons, suffered very severely. The prisoners and loot taken after the fight were conveyed to Ladysmith, which is full of Boer prisoners and wounded. The latter bear terrible evid- ence of the swordmanship of our cavalry. Lyddite is said to have been used on Thu rsday with good effect against the enemy. NEWS FROM KIMBERLEY AND MAFEKING.

A RUNCORN SOLDIER'S EXPERIENCES IN CAPE COLONY.

The following letter has been received by the parents of Lance-Corporal Caulfield. of G Company.lst Battaii:m Royal Minster Fusiliers, dated Octob-r 14th, from the Orange River Stag: 13, Cape Colony. " We were travel- ling in train for three eays, from Stellinboch, to Orange River. This station is on the border a the Orange Free State. War is declared by the Bers and we are ex- pe,it'ng an attack any minute. They have att4cked an armour-plated train,and killed 15 men and an officer. We are up watching day and night. We are only allowel to

_ sleep from 12 o'clock noon CO 3 o'clock,and then we have to lie down with our belt and straps, and 100 rounds of ammunition and rifle by our side. We are never allowed to remlve our boots or putees,and our straps are always on. It is very hot here in the daytime, and we are round the hills all night. Every train that comes in the stati.an is searched, and every passenger put under escort until the search has been made. We are havin;?: a lively time of it. When we were in Wynberg I was talking to a fellow belonging to the Liverpoo!s. Fie bMongs to Widnes. He said he knew our family, but I had no tine to say much to him. He did teil air his name, but I forget it. I suppose there is a lot of talk in the papoN about the war, but there are too many lies about it. But it is true Wooly, the train. I W 33 on otr-post and heard the shots. That's nothing to wha.'7, will come yet. It was reported that there w-le 300 Boers coming down the line, but let 'em all come.' We'll give them a warm receptien for keeping U 3 out of bed. Our wills hlve been made out, and put in a little p3ckA, and sewn ita. Cheer up ; we will meet again."

SHUNTING FATALITY AT EARLESTOWN.

A Haydock Man Killed.

Mr. Brighouse, county coroner,h,l I the inquest on thn body of William D avies,aged t wenty-four.of Btanley Miil C.ttage, Ashton,at the Waggon Hotel.thydock,on Mon- day night. Mr. Bland represented the L. and N.W.R. Company, Mr. J. 0. Swift (of Liverpool) represented Messrs. Evans and Co., an there were present Messrs. John Robinson (general manager) and Lothom.— Frederick Ellis, of 18, Eirle-street, Earlestown, sigual- man at Earlestown Station, said that about five minutes to seven on Friday night ha received a message that a Haydock C)lliery train was coming to his box to eater the siding. He went to some tumbler points to turn the train on to the middle siding. The train came on with deceased sitting on the buffer of the first waggon. It proved that a waggon was standing foul of the points leading into the siding, and the coal train ran into this waggon- The corner of Daviea's waggon struck the ebstructing waggon and his log was jammed fast by the buffer. He was shochingly injured, and it was sometime before he could be re- leased.—The Coroner asked the witness if he went to look if the points were clear. —Ellis : No.—Mr. Bland asks dto be allowed to explain that the brakesman had to perform certain ditties. It was his duty not to ride on the waggon, but to walk in front and see that eve:y- thing was clear.—Ellie, in answer to the Coroner, said that if the waggon had not been in the way the points that he turned would have allowed the coal train to go safely on to theline.—Mr. Swift said that Messrs. Evans and Co. did not feel that they were civilly or criminally interested.—The Coroner: You see, Mr. Bland, this man is killed on your line through the fact that this waggon is foul of your points.—After considerable dis- cussion, the Coroner said he was prepared to advise the jury that they had only to return a verdict of "Acci- dental death." They had nothing to do with any civil liability,and no one was criminally to blame.—The jury returned a verdict accordingly.

ENGINEER'S SCEIENIE.

The proposed new waterwerks will consist of a pump- ing station with duplicate pumping engines and steam boilers, covered tank, rising main, covered service re- servoir, and elevated tank, to be carried out in three sections, of which sections numbers 1 and 2 are already authorized. Section No. 1 will include two beretioles with horinntal engine actuating a pump in each of the bereholee, and di,chare.ing 15,000 gallons per hour into a covered tank to hold 200,000 gallons, and a separate qeadruple acting force pump drawing from this tank sal delivering a maximum quantity of 24,000 gallons per heur into the district through an 8-inca main cen- fleeted to the exi,ting 6-inch main at Newton Bridge. This tank would allow of the lift pumps in horeholes be- ing worked continually night and day at a regular ewe 1, ani the force pump delivering unto the district could be set to varying speed according to the maximum require- ment during the day or the minimum during the night, while in the cese of fire this pump could be used to in- crease the pressure in the mains. Tne necessary build- ings for the above would be erected at this stage. Sec- tion No. 2 consists of a well 11 feet by 6 feet and 200 feet deep.. with (say) 500 fe,-it of turtnelikg 6 feet high and 5 feet wide, and the tunnel connected to each bore- hetes with a hesdine, and to place over the well a com- pound engine working lift and force pumps capable of dealing with (say) 20,000 gallons per hour. A cottage would now te erected for the man in charge. This section. which is necessary along wl:le the fi-st sec- tion to make in themselves a complete waterworks' scheme, would place the district in possession of a water supply with the least possible delay at an eetnneted expenditure of £13,000, when the waterworks would then be in a safe and cemplete sate able to ' cope with Ray emergency of accident or fire in addition to a plenti- fed and regular supply. The third section would con- sice• of a service reservoir capable of clntaining at least 500,000 eal!ons, and a high level tank conttining 25,000 gallons 50 feet from the ground, and both cnnnectFd with the pumping station by a 12-inch main. His total o3timee of the cost was £22.000. CLESIIK'S ES ['DILATED EXPENDITURE IN RESPECT OF THE SCHEME.

Imm-diatel7 upon completion of the Waterworks it is estimated that at least 100,000,000 gallons of water will be supplid to and consumed in the district pt annutn Oast year's actual supply and co:sumption was 72,000,000 gallons of water), and upon this estimate an approximate annual expenditurs in respect of whit is termed in the Engineer's Roport the " first and second sections, was £1,524 Ss 103, thowin:.t tho cost of water on the said estimated consumption of 100,000,000 gallons to be 36531. per 1,000 gallons per annum in res- pect of the first two sections, Toe annual estimated expelditure in respect of the third section was £2,366 I3s 10d. Assuming that the third section will not 131 proceeded with until such a period as the consumption of water will have reached 150,000,000 alions per annum, then the cost per 1,000 gallons would be 3 7861. There would be an estimated saving f £975 lls 2d per annum on sections one and two, and £1,393 6s 61 on section 3. MEDICAL OFFICERS' QUARTERLY REPORT.

Dr. Watkins presented his quarterly report for the quitter ended September 30 at the meeting of the High- ways and Sanitary Committee. There were registered 144 births, 89 deaths, excess of births 55. The birth rate was 381 and the death rate 23'5 per thousand of the estimated lypulation. The infantile deaths were at an annual rate of 30'3 per cent ; the deaths of children under 5 years of age were 662 per cent ot the total deaths ; and the zymotic death rate was 2 4 per 1,000 of the estimated population. 31 cases of infectious sicanees were notified ; 10 in Viaduct Ward ; 4 in Town Hall Ward ; 1 in Crorr-lane Ward ; 8 in Wargrave Ward; and ia Newton Ward. He greatly regretted to record that only six of these cases from three houses were received into the Isolation Hospital, thus leaving 19 houses the foci of fever infection. Ha could not gauge the measure of this calamity to the suspension of the power of i,olation, happening, especially as it did in the hot days of August, when summer diarrhea was extremely prevalent in the district. No infectious case had been received into the Isolation Hos- pital from this district since the 7th of July last. In corroboration of his opinion about isolation, or rather the want of it, he was able to offer independent testi- mony. Dr. Hayward, medical officer of health, of Hay- dock, recently wrote him " On August 22nd last I noti- fied to you formally the existence of a case of typhoid fever at 103. Legh-street, Eerlestown, in the person of a man named E lw krd Roberts—at the same time I recom- mended that the case should be removed to the Isolation Hospital. This was in view of these facOs : (1) The man was lodging in a room occupied by several other men. (2) The only person to give him any attendance was an elderly woman, the landlady, who has a green- grocer's shop to look after. (3) The landlady and her husband were anxious to have the man removed, and the man himself desired to go. However, I was in- formed that it was impossible to have the man removed. The man, fortunately, has recovered, bat during the course of his illness he several times, while delirious,got up and went into the street. From the point of view of the public it is scarcely satisfactory that the occupa- tions of nurse of a typhoid patient and server of green- groceries should be combined in the same person. My object in addressing this communication to you is that you may be able to represent to your sanitary authority, by means of a concrete case, the danger of having no adequate provision fair the isolation of cases of infectious diseases." In concluding his repert Dr. Watkins said he was glad to report a satisfactory improvement in the effluent from the Newton Mere in the last quarter, and also from observation that the water flowing past the Vulcan manifests, both visibly and otherwise, a consi- derable diminution of the pollution which had pertained for a long time past. The Highways and Sanitary Committee ordered that the Medical Officer of Health be requested to examine the printed no`ic.s of precautions against infectious disease, and revise them with the latest information. A let!er dated the sth October, was read from the War- rington Corporation stating that they could not at pre- sent enter into an arrangement, as suggested, with re- ference to reserving in the Warrington Isolation Hospital a specific number of beds for the absolute use of this Council, but that as plans for a considerable extension of the hospital had been approved by the local Govern- ment Board, consequently the Health Committee of the Corporation hoped in a very abort time to be in a better po.ithn for dealing with cases from this district. It was ordered that the Clerk again write to the Warring- ton Corporation astring them to reserve when the exten- sion of their hospital is completed a specific number (not less than six) of beds for cases of emergency from this district, in addition to the right of this Council to send cases as at present.
